287. **No log line for each call of the monitor.** `programs()` and `counts()` wrote one line at each call; the check of the count of rows now calls `monitor.count("programs")` (`rows_call`). `counts()` has the scheduling strategy (its number, and its name as the unit) and its changes: 15 rows.
288. **The compositor is a service**, with its exports in `programs/compositor/exports.odin` and four lines in `main.odin`: the service starts after "compositor: ok", the wait list has the service, and the calls run after the input records, so that `pointer()` gives the place after the last move. The root service starts the compositor itself, not with `start()`, so it gives the service ring and the file there, and puts the compositor in its table of started programs, where a connection finds a service by its name; `stop("compositor")` stays refused.
289. **`pointer()` is a Table of one row**, not the Record that the coordinator asked: a Record result has no error record (section 4 of `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`), and the place of the pointer is a fact of the screen of the person (P20, line 6), so a caller that does not act for the person must get a refusal, not zeros. `pointer()` also has the menu that is open and the launcher; `windows()` has the focus and whether the keys go to the window (not while the launcher is open).
290. **Where `remote` starts in the main scenario**: after `workers`, by a blind launch. Not at the start: the compositor places each new window by the count of the windows before it, and the first part of the scenario presses at fixed places inside windows that it did not move; one more window before them moves each of them. Not in the root service of a test build: the system under test must be the system that ships. The persistence machines and the machine of a failed start do not run `remote`: their presses stay blind.
291. **The tool keeps each module file and its hash** for a client: a call read and hashed the file each time, 3.4 ms of the 3.7 ms of a confirmation of the pointer. Now a confirmation takes 257 us at least and 299 us in the middle (the last full check).
292. **The log is a service**: the program `log` holds the grant to read the log records, so it serves them, as the monitor serves the reports. `records(after)` gives rows of 256 bytes, the largest record of a description: the text keeps 168 of its 176 bytes. A caller reads from a number, and the first row says how many records the kernel wrote over in between.
293. **The step `Record`** waits for a log record through `log.records`: the program, the channel and the level must be equal, and the text must have a part. 11 checks moved, where the program and the level add to the text (the root service, the block services, `disk`, `storage`). The other checks stay `.Wait` on the serial port: it works before `remote` runs and when the network does not.
294. **No Record argument from the host**: no export that `remote` reaches takes a Record, so the parser has Integers and Texts only.

295. **TCP is a library in the program** (`support/tcp`, `docs/NETWORK.md`; property 5, its dated note, and the direction of the owner "UDP, TCP, QUIC, a server"). The state of each connection, the windows, the timers, the retransmissions, the congestion control and the reassembly are in the program, in records that it owns. The kernel did not change. The library is pure code over memory of the caller: no kernel operation, no allocation, so the kernel tool runs it on the host as the TCP of the peer, and `odin test support/tcp` has 15 tests (a link with loss, reorder and duplicates; the wrap of 2^32; the timer of RFC 6298 and its doubling to 60 s; a fast retransmit with no timeout; a zero window; RST and challenge ACKs; a close from the two sides at once; the error records).
296. **A segment endpoint, not a new contract** (`contract/datagram`). A TCP endpoint has the ring, the region, the commands and the error records of a datagram endpoint; its grant has the flag TCP, and a Send or a Receive carries a whole segment (20 to 1480 bytes). One mechanism for one concept: an endpoint carries IPv4 payloads of one protocol for one program. The grant is one value with two more flags (`GRANT_TCP`, `GRANT_LISTEN`); `grant_unpack` gives a record (`datagram.Grant`). `remote` and `net` read it.
297. **Two forms of TCP grant** (P20, lines 3, 4 and 11). A client grant names one remote; the network service gives a block of 64 local ports (a closed connection stays in TIME-WAIT, and a local port serves one connection to one remote at a time). A listen grant names one local port, which the root service chooses; the program answers only the admitted remotes: a remote that sent a segment to the port. The network service keeps 64 for each listen grant; the program releases one when its connection ends (`Release`), and a RST to a segment of no connection releases it too. A program with a listen grant cannot open a connection to a machine that did not come to it. A full table drops a segment from a new remote and counts it in the problems of the endpoint (kind `Full`). A listen port cannot be a port of another segment endpoint or of the blocks of client ports.
298. **Where the checksums are computed and checked.** The library computes the TCP checksum of each segment that it sends; the network service does not check it (a wrong checksum harms only the program that wrote it). The network service checks the TCP checksum of each segment that arrives, as it checks UDP (choice 198), because it reads the ports to choose the endpoint: a port that the wire damaged must not give a segment to another program. The coordinator proposed "the library", and this step changed it for that reason. A refused segment is a problem row: Too_Short, Bad_Header, Bad_Length, Bad_Checksum of the layer `Tcp`.
299. **What the network service does on the data path for TCP**: the same work as for UDP. It copies each segment one time (between the region and its frame), reads the ports in its own copy, checks them against the grant, writes the IPv4 header, and checks the TCP checksum of a segment that arrives. It keeps no sequence number and no window. So the dated note of property 5 is right about the place of the protocol, and not complete about the data path. `docs/NETWORK.md`, "The note of property 5", has the wording that the coordinator can record.
300. **The interface of the library is data-oriented** (owner, 2026-11-10, item 94 of `HANDOFF.md`). A connection is one record of 400 bytes (`tcp.Connection`); the caller gives the table of records and one block of memory for the buffers, and the buffers of a connection are at a place that its index gives. `tcp.step` takes all the segments that arrived, the time, the free places of the output and a table of events, and gives the deadline of the next timer: one call for a batch, no callback, no blocking call. An event is a record of 8 bytes: one for each connection with news. `connect`, `write`, `read`, `close` and `abort` change one record; an error is an `errors.Error_Record` (units `Connections` and `Tcp_Ports`, new). The program reads each field of its connections with no call: `server` draws them.
301. **The rules of TCP that the library follows** (`docs/NETWORK.md` has the table and the sources): RFC 9293 for the states, the windows and the RST; RFC 6528 for the initial sequence numbers (BLAKE3 of a secret and the four values, plus a counter of 4 us); RFC 6298 for the timer, with a least timeout of 200 ms, not 1 s (the round trips of the link are under 1 ms); RFC 5681 Reno, with the partial ACK of NewReno (RFC 6582) and Limited Transmit (RFC 3042); RFC 5961 for the RST, the SYN and the ACK out of the window. Measured on the host, 1 MiB with 5 % loss in each direction: 5 to 10 s with Reno and NewReno alone, 1.5 to 2.7 s with Limited Transmit (6 seeds). TIME-WAIT is 10 s (an MSL of 5 s for one link), not the 4 minutes of RFC 9293.
302. **Faults that the tests found in the library before this commit, for the record.** (1) `recover` of NewReno started at 0, not at the initial sequence number: with an initial number above 2^31, no fast retransmit could start. (2) A batch of segments after a hole got one ACK, not one for each segment (RFC 5681 4.2): with batches, the sender never saw three duplicate ACKs. The record now counts the ACKs that it owes. (3) Avoidance of the silly window could stop a sender with a small window and nothing in flight: the persist timer now ends that case too. (4) The congestion window grew with no end when the window of the remote limited the sender: it now stops at the largest window that the remote gave.
303. **The secret of the initial sequence numbers comes from `rdrand`** (`tcp.secret_make`), an instruction that a program runs with no handle; with no `rdrand`, from the cycle counter, which an attacker can guess. The log says which. The system has no random service: a gap.
304. **A small HTTP/1.1 for the test** (`support/web`): the head of a request and of a response, Content-Length, "Connection: close", the page `/large` whose bytes come from their offset, and FNV-1a of 64 bits. `server`, `fetch` and the kernel tool share it, so the three agree on the hash of 1 MiB: e7867887f2822325. Two host tests compare the hash with the one of `core:hash`.
305. **The program `server`**: one thread, a table of 32 connections with 64 KiB to send and 32 KiB to receive each (3 MiB of memory of the program), `GET /` (a page of its own counts: connections, requests, bytes, retransmissions, the time since its start, its kernel entries), `GET /large`, `POST /sink`. The time of an answer ends at the ACK of its last byte. A connection that this side closed is on the log when it enters TIME-WAIT, not 10 s later. Its window (`docs/DESIGN.md`, the colors of `support/graphics/rules.odin`): three cards (the listen grant with the count of open connections in the large size, the traffic with the count of answered requests, the measures), and the table of connections: the remote in the color of the machine, the state in the color of its phase (the boot program while it opens, the program while it runs, the kernel in the FIN states and TIME-WAIT), the bytes, the round trip, the congestion window as a bar whose width is its size against 64 KiB, the retransmissions and the probes; then the connections that ended, dim. One draw took 250 to 639 us.
306. **The program `fetch`**: a client grant of 10.0.2.100 port 80, eight cases with their answers, as `net` shows its cases. Two cases send a segment that the grant does not allow, written by hand (`tcp.header_write`), to show that the network service checks the copy and refuses it with the grant in the completion. `fetch` starts from the console (`start("fetch")`), not from the menu: it has a use only on the machine with libslirp.
307. **The peer is a TCP client that the tool drives** (`tools/kerneltool/peer_tcp.odin`, step `Peer`). It runs `support/tcp` in its thread, between frames: its socket waits 1 ms at most. It chooses the UDP port where QEMU receives the frames of the machine (`local.port` of `-netdev dgram`), so it can send the first SYN before the machine sends a frame. It reads each TCP frame of the machine with its own code, also the checksum. The cases change frames with a generator that each case seeds: each run drops, reorders and repeats the same frames. The cost: the library tests itself in these cases; the machine with libslirp tests it against another implementation.
308. **The independent stack is libslirp, through QEMU user networking** (`-netdev user,restrict=on`, a third network machine). `hostfwd` gives a free port of the loopback of the host to the port 80 of the machine; `guestfwd` gives 10.0.2.100 port 80 to a listener of the tool (a QEMU character device that connects when QEMU starts, so the listener reads with no time limit). The TCP of the machine talks to the one of libslirp; libslirp talks to the TCP of the host. A second network device on one machine was not needed: a second machine is one more start, and the frame service and the network service did not change. libslirp acknowledges the FIN of a connection of `guestfwd` and does not send its own: `fetch` stays in FIN-WAIT-2, and its case says it.
309. **Measured** (the machine test after the rebase on the step 46, QEMU with KVM, 4 processors, while other machine tests ran on the host; one run unless said). Between the peer and `server`: 1 MiB from the server in 52.9 ms (19.8 MB/s), 1 MiB to the server at 20.2 MB/s; with 5 % loss in each direction 0.8 and 0.7 MB/s (48 segments sent again; 2.2 and 0.8 MB/s in an earlier run); with 10 % reordered 16.6 and 15.3 MB/s; with 10 % repeated 16.1 and 14.9 MB/s; a zero window of 1.5 s: 4 probes. The handshake seen by `server` (SYN to the ACK of its SYN, ACK): 61 to 135 us at least. `GET /` answered and acknowledged in 157 to 224 us. With libslirp: the host fetched 1 MiB at 58.6 to 64.9 MB/s and sent 1 MiB at 57.6 to 61.0 MB/s (the clock of the host); `server` measured 92.9 to 98.4 MB/s out and 81.9 to 90.4 MB/s in; `fetch` measured the handshake in 261 to 266 us, 1 MiB in at 105.3 to 140.7 MB/s, 1 MiB out at 11.4 to 11.9 MB/s, and `GET /hello` in 42.6 to 43.5 ms, a cause that this step did not find (the tool answers at once; the time is in QEMU, libslirp or the wait of `fetch`). In the case of 16 connections, the answers were acknowledged 0.2 to 30 ms after their request, in the order of the connections: the peer runs one step for each frame. The window of a connection is 32 KiB (the receive buffer), so one connection cannot exceed 32 KiB for each round trip. The test asserts no speed.
310. **Where the copy and the fill came from.** With `-no-crt`, the Odin runtime (`base/runtime/procs.odin`) defines `memcpy`, `memmove`, `memset` and `bzero` with a strong linkage in the object of each module. `memcpy` and `memmove` are loops of one byte with the attributes `noinline optnone` in the LLVM IR: the code keeps its counter in memory. `memset` is a loop of one byte that LLVM makes a loop of 32 bytes. A symbol of another object cannot replace a strong symbol of the same link. The call sites in the linked modules, from the compiled code:

| Module | `memcpy` | `memmove` | `memset` |
|---|---|---|---|
| compositor | 4 | 5 | 7 |
| console | 35 | 30 | 79 |
| kernel | 14 | 8 | 32 |
| root | 22 | 9 | 16 |
| storage | 24 | 7 | 21 |

311. **The system has its own copy and fill** (`runtime/memory.asm`): `copy_bytes_without_overlap`, `copy_bytes_with_overlap`, `fill_bytes_with_value`. The kernel tool links the file in each module and in the kernel (`RUNTIME_MEMORY` in `tools/kerneltool/modules.odin`), and in the boot program (the Win64 form), and builds them without `-no-crt`. No program and no kernel file changed for it. The compiler calls the three functions by the names of the C library: these names are aliases in a part of their own, and the code of the system uses the long names (owner, 2026-11-11: no short names such as `memcpy`).
312. **The form of each size, by measure** on the host (AMD Ryzen 7 5800X: ERMS and FSRM; the guest under KVM says the same, the compositor writes it), in ns for one call, the destination 3 bytes after an alignment:

| Size | `rep movsb` | loop of 64 bytes | `rep stosb` | fill loop of 64 bytes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 512 | 13.1 | 8.5 | 26.9 | 7.3 |
| 2048 | 27.5 | 31.0 | 37.1 | 28.1 |
| 4096 | 48.4 | 64.0 | 51.0 | 61.1 |
| 65536 | 986 | 1265 | 515 | 978 |
| 524288 | 10109 | 11130 | 4438 | 8441 |
| 1 MiB | 43463 | 21160 | 38593 | 16855 |

The forms: under 16 bytes, general registers; 16 to 64 bytes, two or four moves of 16 bytes that overlap (all reads before all writes, so they also serve `copy_bytes_with_overlap`); then the loop of 64 bytes up to 2 KiB for the copy and 4 KiB for the fill; `rep movsb` and `rep stosb` up to 1 MiB; the loop again from 1 MiB. `copy_bytes_with_overlap` copies down in blocks of 16 bytes when the destination is after the source in the count, and uses `rep movsb` when it is before. The registers: xmm0 to xmm4, no AVX: `fxsave` does not save the upper halves of the ymm registers. The kernel uses these xmm registers in the file, as its compiled code already does: it saves the registers of a program at each entry (`fxsave`) and runs with the interrupts off, except in `idle_wait`. The rule of the direction flag is now in `docs/MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, section 4: clear at each call; the kernel clears it at `syscall` (`MSR_SFMASK`) and at each interrupt (`cld`).
313. **Tests on the host**: the tool assembles the file with `-DHOST` (the long names only) and `odin test runtime` links it: each size 0 to 300, each alignment 0 to 15 of the source and of the destination, the moves with 33 distances both ways (0, 1 to 17, 31, 63 to 65, half the count, the count and one more), the sizes where the form changes (2047 to 2049, 4095 to 4097, 1 MiB less 1 to 1 MiB and 13), and the fill with three values. A fault put on purpose (no write of the first 16 bytes of a move down) fails the tests.
314. **`graphics.copy` is a plain copy again**, and the compositor measures at its start, in the guest, ns for one call (8, 64, 512, 4096, 65536 bytes and a frame less 128 bytes, aligned): `copy_bytes_without_overlap` 1.5, 1.9, 9.9, 34.6, 923, 73765; the loop of 16 bytes of the step before 3.5, 19.6, 149, 1170, 19856, 956365; `fill_bytes_with_value` 2.8, 3.3, 7.6, 42.6, 502, 44292. Before and after on one `master` (`9cf18a6` before, the same with this step after; the load of the host was 3 to 15 during the run after):

| Measure | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| A title bar of 1024 pixels, one row then copies | 5.7 us | 1.7 us |
| A full frame, 12 windows or more | 1648 us (5 frames) | 1679 us (13 frames) |
| A frame of the move of `life` / `boot` | 269 / 1269 us | 250 / 1357 us |
| The first use of a page (`frames`) | 830 ns | 713 ns |
| Pages on first use in one second, 1 / 2 / 4 lockers (`locks`) | 1.24 / 2.32 / 3.47 million | 1.63 / 2.84 / 4.23 million |
| A call of the host to `monitor.count`, in the middle | 189 us | 169 us |
| The round trip of a datagram (`net`), in the middle | 83 us | 111 us |
| A burst of `net`, datagrams in one second | 95 149 | 60 796 |
| The boot program loads the kernel and the boot image | 19.5 / 20.1 ms | 22.6 / 23.2 ms |

The last check after the rebase on `bec7cfa` (load 6 to 13) gave: the first use of a page 676 ns; 1.90 / 2.68 / 4.19 million pages on first use; the call of the host 170 us; the round trip of `net` 73 us; the burst 84 319. TCP, from `server` and the tool (before: the numbers of choice 309, 19.4 MB/s with the peer and 99 MB/s with libslirp): `server` received 1 MiB from the peer at 27.2, 1.3, 22.0 and 19.2 MB/s in one run; the host through libslirp fetched at 79.9 MB/s and sent at 90.8 MB/s. TCP did not gain a measurable part: it is bound by the network path, and one run gives values from 1.3 to 27.2 MB/s.

The frames do not change: a frame is bound by the writes to the framebuffer of QEMU (3 MiB in about 1.7 ms), not by the copy in memory. The kernel gains on the first use of a page (14 %, 31 % more pages in one second with one locker). The network and the boot program measured worse in the run after, under a load of the host 5 times higher; in the run of the step before on the old `master` with this change and a low load, the round trip was 67 us, the burst 94 443, the call 148 us and the load of the boot program 15.6 to 23.5 ms on 17 machines. These four measures are not conclusive on one run each.
315. **The palettes of the programs**: 20 programs take the colors that have a role of the rules from `support/graphics`; the others stay in the programs (`docs/DESIGN.md`, "State"). No layout changed. The places of new windows stay off the grid (see the gaps).
316. **One developer tool, `inspect`**, with the machine and the right Inspect, the boot image (the names of the modules and their exports) and a service ring (`grants_of`). It reads reports and changes nothing. It does not read a byte of the memory of another program: the reports give counts, places, states and the stack trace. That stronger right (P20, line 2) is not added; the hex view shows the memory of `inspect` itself (`docs/TOOLS.md`).
317. **Four reports of the kernel** for a developer tool, each read-only, through `Inspect` (`contract/operation/operation.odin` lines 370 to 445; `kernel/report.odin` lines 103 to 225; the cases in `kernel/enter.odin` line 715 and 746; three buffers of the processor record, `kernel/processor.odin` line 161; the sizes, `kernel/diagnose.odin` line 563). `Program_Mappings`: each mapping of a program with the pages that have a frame (read from the page table, as `present_pages` does), its memory object and flags. `Threads`: the state, the processor, the home queue and the fields of the strategy of each thread, read with no run lock (each field whole). `Stop`: the stop report of a slot. `Pages`: which of 64 pages from an address have a frame, so that a reader of bytes gives no frame by its read; slot 0 is the program that asks. The reports are built in the processor record under the control lock and the program lock of the program, then copied to the caller with no lock of another program. `kernel/memory.odin` and `kernel/paging.odin` are not changed: the reports call `page_entry` with no creation.
318. **The kind of a mapping** comes from facts: the module record that holds its address (code with Execute, data with Write, constants else), Device, Thread_Area, Shared (another handle or mapping names the memory object), else memory of the program. The kernel does not know a stack: a stack is memory of the program.
319. **The band of an address space** (`support/addresses`, with unit tests on the host): each mapping has 4 pixels and a part of the rest in proportion to its pages; a gap between two mappings is a folded gap of 16 pixels with lines at an angle (`DESIGN.md`, section 2). An address is `module + offset` in the module record that holds it, and a frame of a stack trace adds the near export: the export with the highest address at or below it.
320. **Live samples, and their cost.** A sample each 250 ms: the program report of each of the 64 slots (the list) and five reports of the chosen program, 68 kernel entries in all. In the last full check, after the count of the present pages in each mapping (324): 33 to 62 us (a sample of the compositor, 55 mappings and 8319 pages: 48 us; of `threads`, 18 mappings: 35 us); before it, with the walk of the page table: 95 to 143 us, and 1.5 ms for the first sample with cold caches. A value that changed since the sample before is drawn in the color of the boot program for one sample. The kernel time of the mappings report grows with the mapped pages of the program: it reads one entry of the page table for each page.
321. **The same facts as exports**: `inspect.mappings(program)`, `inspect.threads(program)`, `inspect.modules(program)`, Tables with error records, for a caller that acts for the person; `callable_of("remote")` has `inspect`.
322. **`errors` keeps the program `fault` that stopped** until the next run of its case: the slot of a program that stopped ends with its last handle, and the developer tool shows its stop report while it lives.
323. **The test**: the step `Agree` compares two tools on two reports of the kernel (`monitor.programs()` and `inspect.mappings()`, `inspect.threads()`); the scenario starts `monitor` again before it, because the scenario revoked its machine grant before. `kerneltool test main` runs the main machine only.
324. **Corrections after an independent read of the kernel part.** (F1) `Pages` answers for the program that asks only: about another program it was a side channel, page by page; an index that is not 0 gives `Bad_Operation` with the error record Bad_Value of the argument 3, and an address whose 64 pages pass the end of the user half is refused; the case takes no control lock (the program lock of the caller is held). (W1) The pages with a frame are a count of each mapping (`Mapping.present_page_count`, `kernel/program.odin`): `map_region` sets it, `page_give` adds 1 when an entry becomes present, `unmap` ends it, each under the program lock that the path holds; `present_pages` of `Program_Report` and the mappings report read it in place of a walk of the page table with no bound, under the control lock. The test build checks it at each unmap. (W2) The stop report of a free slot is zero, not the stop of its old program. (W4) `Program_Report`, `Module_Report`, `Stop_Report` and the two new reports have the serial of the program; `inspect` drops a sample of two programs and chooses by slot and serial. (W5) The stop report through Inspect keeps only the frames in a module record of the program, and the address of the fault only in the first page or a module record (`docs/TOOLS.md`). (N2) Each export of `inspect` checks that the caller acts for the person; the network machine shows the three refusals after `revoke("remote", "person")`. The click on the list is a step of the test.
379. **The color of an error record in the window of `remote`**: an answer whose export gave an error record is drawn in the amber of the rules (`COLOR_BOOT_PROGRAM`), the color that the program `log` gives to the level Warning: the call ran and the service gave its failure as a value that the caller can act on, so it is not the color "failed" and not the green of a result. A refusal of `remote` itself ("refused by remote") and of the service keep the color "failed": no call ran.
380. **The refusals of `Pages` are cases of `errors`**, with the sentences of their error records: `t` "Inspect: the argument index has the value 1, which the call refuses" (the pages of another program), `u` "Inspect: the argument address has the value 140737488351232, which the call refuses; it takes at most 140737488093184" (the 64 pages from the first page pass the end of the user half). The kernel explains them in `value_explain`, the path of `Bad_Operation`. `errors` has a second page of cases (Tab): its window keeps the height of 19 cases.
381. **`log.records` cuts no byte.** A row of a description has 256 bytes at most, so the text of a row has 160 bytes: a log record of up to 176 bytes gives two rows with one number, and a row says `continues = 1` when its text goes on in the next row (the rest of the record, or the next record when the kernel split the text, `Log_Record.more`). The step `Record` joins the rows of one text: the line of `errors` under contention, 2 log records, comes back as one text from 3 rows.
382. **A disk that loses what was not flushed**, served by the kernel tool (`network_block_device.odin`): QEMU reaches the data disk through the Network Block Device protocol (fixed newstyle handshake, simple replies, read, write, flush and the flag FUA; about 300 lines with `core:net`). The tool keeps two images: what the machine reads (each write), and what is durable (each write before the last flush, and each FUA write), and the writes after the last flush in order. Chosen over `blkdebug` of QEMU: a rule of `blkdebug` can drop writes, but the tool would not know which writes the machine made; with NBD the tool is the model of a real disk and needs nothing of QEMU but its NBD client.
383. **The loss of power at the write of a root.** A loss at a moment of the clock nearly never falls between a write and its flush (each commit is two writes and two flushes, QEMU joins the blocks of one write). So the disk loses its power at the first write of a root (blocks 0 and 1) after a seeded count of writes: the hardest moment, when the blocks of the commit are durable and its root is not. A loss at a write of data blocks is a subset: those blocks are free for the durable root.
384. **A search, with no machine for each image.** The open of a data disk (the choice of the root, the walk, the check of each block) moves from the engine to `support/storage_mount`, which the storage service and the tool both run. The tool checks each image: it opens; no block has a wrong check, no block is named two times, the count of blocks of each store is what its table names (two new counts of the open; the service writes them on the log if they are not 0); the objects are those of the durable image or those of the image with every write, byte for byte, never a mix. It found no fault of the engine. The engine asks the flushes at the right moments (its blocks, then its root), and the block service passes each one to the device (VIRTIO_BLK_T_FLUSH): the disk received two flushes for each durable commit.
385. **A ring completion that fails holds an error record in its values** (`errors.completion_record`), the form of the completion of the kernel: block, frame, datagram, the control ring of the network service and the root ring. It replaces the values of each status of choice 199. A remote and its port are one value (`errors.endpoint_pack`, the unit `Endpoint`). New kinds: No_Answer, Unsupported, Link_Down, Busy, Out_Of_Range, Reserved; new units: Requests, Endpoint, Region, Root_Commands, Services, Grants, Display, Terminal. The check of a data region of a device ring is one procedure (`system.region_refusal`) for the block service and the frame service.
386. **The root service finds the cause of a refusal after it**, as `kernel/diagnose.odin` does (`refusal`): the start, stop and connect procedures keep their status, and one procedure reads the state again. A cause that the state does not show now is Refused, with the status. The console writes the sentence of the record in place of a text that it guessed from the status (it said "no program has the name" for each refusal of Stop). Replace keeps the texts of the console for its statuses (a chain, a full table, a module that replaces itself), which its contract documents.
387. **A wrapper writes the record of the kernel operation that failed in it, or its own** (`system.wrapper_error_text`): a record with an operation is the kernel's, a record with the operation 0 names an argument of the wrapper (`display.OPEN_ARGUMENTS`). `display.open` now refuses a size of 0 or above 2048 and a title of more than 31 bytes, which it cut before; no program of the system passes one.
388. **An export that refuses must give a negative result for it**: `paint.line` and `paint.write` give an Integer now (the lines drawn, the bytes of the text), and `tally.add` keeps the count, which it never takes below 0: an amount that would is refused (Too_Small). Before, a wrong color was taken modulo 6, a place off the paper was dropped with no answer, and a long note was cut.
389. **The sentence of Bad_Value says "it takes at most N"**: "1 to N" was wrong for the arguments whose first value is 0 (an index of a connection, an offset, a color).

325. **The close button is the concept "stop a program"** (owner, 2026-11-11: "et ce serait bien que les x de fermeture soit rouge, riche"; the coordinator chose the nearest form that keeps "one color is one concept"). A deep red `COLOR_STOP_PROGRAM` (`0xB82232`), not the coral of "failed": a block from 118 % at the top to 82 % at the bottom, a top line at 150 %, the `x` of the font in white; brighter (`0xE0343F`) when the pointer is over it, which the compositor draws again at each change; quiet (`0x4E2229`, a dim pink `x`) on a window that is not in front. A cross of squares of 2 pixels was tried beside the character: its two lines do not meet on one square at the centre; the character stays (the two forms are in the devlog of 2026-11-11, `close_preview`). The center of the button does not move: the machine test presses the same place. No program has a button that stops another program: `rings` shows the state "stopped" of a node, a state and not a button. `flake.nix` names `runtime/` (the file set of the flake did not see `runtime/memory.asm`).
326. **The interface package** (`support/interface`, `docs/INTERFACE.md`). What the programs draw by hand, counted on `cbe3c0e`: 34 programs, 591 lines with a drawing call, 21 programs with cards, 5 private copies of `card`, 5 of large text, 15 programs with bars, 8 with tables, 3 lists with a choice, 2 text inputs; each program draws its whole surface for each change. The model: immediate mode over draw tables in the memory of the caller (P14): each frame the program describes its window again; each element appends draw records of 40 bytes (format version 1: a rectangle, a vertical or a horizontal gradient, a run of text, a clip, the grid of points) and gives an `Element_Result` as a value. No allocation, no callback, no global state.
327. **The damage by difference**: the package compares the table of the frame with the table of the frame before, record by record at one index (the bytes of the records, the bytes of their texts); a record that differs damages its old and its new rectangle. A record added in the middle damages each record after it: too much, never too little. The host test `damage_is_never_too_small` draws a frame in its damage over the frame before and compares it, pixel for pixel, with the frame drawn whole (the idea of the verify mode of `gui_toolkit`). `display.present_rectangle` gives the compositor the rectangle that changed: before, `Present` damaged the whole surface (a gap of this file). A surface with two pictures is drawn whole.
328. **The cost of the difference**, measured by `interface_elements` on its own window of 68 records, the mean of 100 rounds: nothing changed, 0 rectangles in 0.8 to 0.9 us; one number changed, 3 rectangles in 0.8 us; everything moved, 1 rectangle of 96 % of the window in 1.8 us. The whole frame drawn: 366 to 411 us; the next frame in its damage: 89 to 120 us. The compositor finds its damage by hand at no cost of comparison; the comparison costs about 13 ns for each record, under 1 % of the drawing that it saves.
329. **Layout in one pass**: an `Area` on the grid; a column gives areas down; a row splits an area into columns whose sizes the caller gives before it draws (`Pixels`, `Characters`, `Share`). No tree and no solver: the windows of the system have fixed sizes.
330. **Input and focus**: the display events of a frame make an `Input`; an element that takes input has an identity (the 64-bit FNV-1a hash of a text); the interface state keeps the focus, the element under the pointer, the pressed element and the order of Tab of the last frame. Tab and Shift+Tab move the focus, Enter and Space press a button, Enter activates a list or a text input, up and down move a choice, a text input takes the characters and Backspace. 12 host tests: the layout, the difference, the text measure, the keyboard, the pointer, the text input and the list.
331. **Three users**, each with no direct drawing call left. `clock` (32 drawing calls to 0): a card of the rules around the same facts (816 x 312 pixels); one picture in 11.6 us, the mean of 10, against 109.2 us before (37 records, 1 damage rectangle: the time since boot; build 2.7 us, difference 1.3 us, draw 4.9 us). The window of `remote` (31 calls to 0): the same three cards; the old view of the first card cut the legend of its bar, which the package showed: the card is 16 pixels higher, the window 528. One picture in 160 us against 173 us before (105 records, 1 damage rectangle: most of its values change at each picture). The launcher of the compositor (48 lines of `launcher_draw` to 24 lines of `launcher_build`): a draw table damaged by difference, given to the damage list of the compositor; its text input takes the characters and Enter through the package. A frame while it is open: 182 to 241 us against 375.7 us before.
332. **The program `interface_elements`**: each element live (a button, a list with a choice, a text input, two bars, lines of a label and a value, a table, the large number of a card), the draw table of the last frame (records, bytes of text, damage rectangles, the time of the build, of the difference and of the drawing), and a button that shows the damage of each frame as outlines (records of the next frame, so a damage that stays does not damage again). The scenario reaches each element with the keyboard: Tab and Enter press the button, Tab and down move the list, Tab and "hello" fill the text input. Pictures: `interface_elements`, `interface_damage`.
333. **A vector font, as research** (`docs/INTERFACE.md`, section 9; Spleen 8x16 stays, owner, 2026-11-10). The Slug patent (US 10,373,352) was disclaimed into the public domain on 2026-03-17 by its author, and reference shaders are free to use with credit (verified on 2026-11-11, sources in the note). The prototype `support/vector_font`: a parser of a subset of TrueType that checks each read (host tests with files cut at 345 lengths and 64 files with 64 changed bytes: no fault), a rasterizer with exact area coverage, a subpixel position and color stripes with the filter 8, 77, 86, 77, 8. It uses `f32`, against section 7 of `DESIGN.md`: a version for the system needs fixed point. The program `vector_glyphs` shows one text in Spleen 8x16 and in DejaVu Sans Mono (Bitstream Vera license, `programs/vector_glyphs/FONT_LICENSE`; 343 096 bytes in the boot image) at 13, 16 and 32 pixels. One glyph: Spleen 32 ns; 13 px from its outline 2939 ns in grayscale, 2396 ns on a whole pixel, 5502 ns with color stripes: about 90 times a bitmap glyph, with no cache. A cache of the coverage of each glyph, size and position is not built and not measured. Picture: `vector_glyphs`.

334. **The admitted remotes belong to the network service, and admission is on a SYN** (finding B1 of the hostile read of 295 to 309). Before, any segment admitted its sender, and only some ends sent a `Release`: a host that sent a bare RST from 64 ports closed the listen port until a restart. Now the network service admits a remote only on a SYN with no ACK and no RST, only when a Receive of the endpoint waits for it, and never from 0, a broadcast or multicast address, or its own address (`network.source_valid`). Each admitted place keeps the time of its last segment, in or out; when the table is full, the oldest place with no segment for 60 s serves again (`ADMITTED_IDLE`): a program that forgets a `Release` loses only places with no traffic. 60 s is more than each timer of `support/tcp` and of `server` (TIME-WAIT 10 s, FIN-WAIT-2 20 s, the limits of 10 s; a connection in retransmission sends a segment at least each 60 s, the most of its timer). The program side stays exact too: `server` releases the remote of each segment whose four values have no record after a step (`tcp.find` is public now), a passive open that a RST ends gives the event `Reset`, and a release waits until the Sends of its pass are in the ring and its record has no RST to send (a `Release` before a RST made the service refuse the RST: found by the case `slow`). A segment of a remote that sent no SYN does not reach the program: the RST of RFC 9293 for it does not go, as behind a firewall. Tests: `admitted_remotes`, `sources_that_are_refused` (host); the peer case `leak` (64 ports each of a bare RST, a bare ACK, a SYN then a RST, a SYN with an option that the library refuses; then a page, 106 to 107 ms after the last of the 320 segments); the case `stray` now checks that no RST comes.
335. **A connection that makes no progress ends** (finding B2). `server`: 10 s with no byte of a request on an open connection: a close; a head that is not whole after 10 s: 408 and a close; a body under 4096 bytes in one second after 10 s: a RST; 10 s with no byte read and no byte acknowledged while a request is in progress (a zero window for ever): a RST; a Content-Length over 16 MiB: 413; a FIN while a request is not whole: a close at once. 10 s is long for a request on a link where a round trip is under 1 ms, and short against 48 records. `server` passes its loop at least each second while a connection is open (with no segment and no timer of TCP, it waited with no deadline: found by the case `slow`). The library: a FIN-WAIT-2 timer of 20 s (a RST at its end); `Table.remote_limit`, the open connections of one remote address (24 of 48 in `server`); a SYN to a full table ends the oldest record in TIME-WAIT and is dropped (the remote sends it again in 1 s), else a RST. Test: the peer case `slow`, 32 connections of one host in 7 kinds (a handshake then nothing, half a head, a trickle body, a refused request then no FIN, half a head then a FIN, a zero window for ever, a Content-Length of 999 999 999 999): 24 accepted, 8 refused with a RST, the server ended the last one after 11.1 s, then a page came after 11.1 s. Host tests: `fin_wait_2_ends`, `remote_limit`, `syn_to_a_full_table`.
336. **Faults of the library from the hostile read** (each with a host test that failed before the correction; `support/tcp/hostile_test.odin`). F1: a RST is checked at the sequence that came, not after the cut of the bytes before `RCV.NXT` (RFC 5961 3.2): a RST with data was 1460 times easier to place. F2: 8 probes of a zero window with no segment of the remote after them end the connection (`Timed_Out`). F4: a passive open ends after 3 timeouts of its SYN, ACK (15 s), not 8 (243 s). W1: the ACKs that answer segments out of the window and the challenge ACKs are 10 in one second at most for each connection (one limit for all connections was CVE-2016-5696). W2: a timeout sets `recover` to the highest sequence sent (RFC 6582 3.2, step 4), and outside recovery `recover` follows the acknowledged edge, so that a fast retransmit still starts after 2^31 bytes. W3: the SYN counts in the acknowledged bytes once, by a field (`syn_acknowledged`), not when `SND.UNA` is the initial sequence again after 2^32 bytes. W4: a FIN is taken only in ESTABLISHED, FIN-WAIT-1 and FIN-WAIT-2; a FIN again in TIME-WAIT restarts its timer.
337. **The notes of the read, corrected**: the send window takes no update from an ACK before `SND.UNA`; the inflation of fast recovery stops 3 segments over the largest window of the remote; `syn_sent` moves `send_next` to the ACK when a timeout had set it back; no round trip sample from a SYN or a SYN, ACK that went two times (Karn); `Endpoint_Grant` takes only a grant of the same form (UDP or TCP, client or listen, the same listen port), because the local ports and their check come from `Endpoint_Add`; `rdrand` that gives all ones or two equal values in four is refused (the fault of some AMD processors), and the window of `server` shows "ISN secret: weak" in the color "failed" when the secret comes from the cycle counter. Two faults that the new tests found: a simultaneous open never ended (SYN-RECEIVED sent no ACK for a segment out of the window), and a passive open that a RST ended gave no event. Tests that the read found missing, now present: loss, reorder and duplicates at the wrap of 2^32; a RST with data; the 8 ranges full and one segment over several ranges; a simultaneous open; a SYN to a full table; an option of length 0 and an option past the header. `tcp.Connection` is 424 bytes. 33 host tests.
338. **Commands that the ring does not take wait for the next pass** (finding F3): `server` and `fetch` put each command through `command_push_or_hold`; a full ring keeps it in a table of the program, which the next pass sends first. A Send, a Receive or a Release is never lost, so no place and no admitted remote leaks.
339. **The 43 ms of `GET /hello` through libslirp were a fault of `fetch`.** QEMU now writes each frame of the machine with libslirp, with its time (`filter-dump`), and the tool prints the frames of `GET /hello` after the run. The frames showed the answer 1.9 ms after the request; a time in `fetch` showed the first segment with data 2.0 ms after the start. The exchange loop of `fetch` checked "the answer is whole" only after its next `pump`, and `pump` waited for the next timer: the delayed ACK, 40 ms. Now `GET /hello` takes 2.7 ms; the 1.9 ms that remain are in libslirp and its character device.
340. **The spread of the throughput.** A run of the controlled machine has the case of 5 % loss (0.6 to 2.2 MB/s by design) beside the clean cases. On a quiet host (a load of 1.9 to 2.1) the peer received 1 MiB in 16.3 to 17.9 ms from its first byte to its last one (58.5 to 64.2 MB/s, 4 rounds), while `server` wrote 13.9 to 18.1 MB/s: its time ended at the ACK of its last byte, which the peer delays up to 40 ms (a delayed ACK at the end of the data). The measure was wrong, not the library: `server` now writes the time when the last byte left the program, and the time of its ACK beside it. The peer writes the load of the host (`/proc/loadavg`) beside each round.
341. **A simple HTTP/1.1 server** (owner, 2026-11-11: "faudra faire un simple server http 1.1 après"; RFC 9110, RFC 9112). `server` keeps one thread and one table; its state is records of fixed size, and it allocates nothing. `support/web.request_read` reads a request head as hostile input: each length is checked before a byte is read; a head has 8192 bytes at most (431), 64 fields (431), a target of 1024 bytes (414), a Content-Length of 15 digits (413 over it, 400 when it is not digits or two values differ); a method that it does not know is 501, a transfer coding (also chunked) 501, a version other than HTTP/1.0 and 1.1 505, a folded line, a space before a colon or a control byte 400, HTTP/1.1 with no Host or two Host fields 400, a target that does not start with "/" 400. Host tests: each bound on both sides, each problem, 50 000 heads with random damage. Connections stay open after an answer (HTTP/1.1), unless "Connection: close", or HTTP/1.0 with no keep-alive. The bytes after a head stay in the input of the connection: they are the body, or the next request (pipelining); the answers go in the order of the requests, one at a time for each connection. HEAD gives the head of GET with no body. 405 has an Allow field; PUT and DELETE are refused: the network is not authenticated. A request with a body that the server does not read gets its answer and the end of the connection. No Date field: the system has no clock of the day, and RFC 9110 6.6.1 says that a server with no clock sends none. The content type comes from the end of a name (a table of 9).
342. **What server serves, by its grants only** (P20): its counts as HTML (`/`) and JSON (`/counts.json`), `/large` and `POST /sink` for the tests, and the pages of its private store (P9, grant Store), by name. The server reads its pages into its memory at its start, and serves them from there: a request never waits for the storage service. A person changes them from the console through the exports of the server (P19, grant Serve): `server.put(name, text)`, `server.remove(name)`, `server.pages()` (a Table), `server.served()` (a Record). put and remove change the store first, and only for a caller that acts for the person; a name has 1 to 39 bytes of letters, digits, ".", "-" and "_"; a page has 60 KiB at most (the call region has 64 KiB); 16 pages. The store keeps an object `pages.index` with their names. No shared folder (P8).
343. **The window of server has two tables**: the connections (the state in the color of its phase, the bytes, the round trip, the congestion window as a bar) and the last 18 requests (the method, the target, the status in the color of its class, the bytes, the time from the first byte of the head to the last byte of the answer in the send buffer), with the answers by class of status as bars, and the measures. One draw took 593 to 830 us. The coordinator offered `support/interface` (step 51) for this window; it was finished before that package came, so it keeps its own drawing with the colors of `support/graphics/rules.odin`. Each request is a log record in the channel `requests`, 100 in one second at most (the log limit of the program is 1000): the others are counted, not written.
344. **A SYN to a full table takes the record of the oldest TIME-WAIT at once** (RFC 6191 takes a SYN in TIME-WAIT too). The remote of that record goes to `Table.reclaimed_remotes`, which the program reads after each step to release it (no Closed event comes for it). Before, the SYN was dropped and came again 1 s later: with "Connection: close" each connection held a record for 10 s, and a new connection for each request gave 4 requests in one second with 48 records; 239 to 250 now.
345. **Faults that the tests of this part found and that are corrected**: the peer lost the bytes of a first read past its head buffer of 2048 bytes (1 MiB came short by 5252 bytes when a head and 5 segments came in one read); a release and the RST of its connection in the wrong order, and no wake for the limits (choice 335).
346. **Measured** (the last runs of `kerneltool test tcp`, the load of the host 7.9 to 8.7, at the limit of 8 that the coordinator set: these numbers are high or low by the load). From the TCP of the host through libslirp: 1000 requests on one connection at 2311 to 3230 requests in one second (the clock of the host); `server` measured 2322 to 3249; 300 requests with a new connection each at 239 to 250 requests in one second; `/` in 5.5 to 6.4 ms with the connection, `/large` at 36.7 to 66.9 MB/s. The peer received 1 MiB in 19.3 to 19.8 ms from its first byte to its last one (53.1 to 54.4 MB/s); 58.5 to 64.2 MB/s with a load of 1.9 to 2.1 (choice 340). `fetch`: `GET /hello` in 2.7 to 3.1 ms, of which 1.9 to 2.8 ms are libslirp (the frames).
372. **Bytes must move, in both directions, after the close too** (the read, F3 and W6). A body with no byte in 10 s ends. A body, and the bytes that this server sends, move 4096 bytes in one second over each period of 10 s, or the connection ends with a RST: a rate over each period, not an average from the start (1 MiB at once then nothing held a record 68 minutes; one byte acknowledged each 9 s held an answer for days). "Sending" is an answer in progress, or bytes in the send buffer that the remote did not acknowledge: a page of up to 60 KiB fits in the send buffer, and its request ends at once. The limits run in FIN-WAIT-1, CLOSING and LAST-ACK too. No byte acknowledged in 10 s and a rate under the least one are one fact with one reason on the log ("an answer under 4096 bytes in one second"): which rule sees it first depends on when the first bytes went. Not a limit of the whole answer (`bytes / 4096 + 10 s`, the form that the read offered): the rate over each period gives the same bound and one rule for both directions.
373. **An answer of a page keeps the generation of its place** (the read, B2). Each `put` and `remove` gives the place a new generation; `answer_write` compares it before each copy, and a page that changed ends the connection with a RST: the remote has a prefix of the old page and a RST, never bytes of two pages under one `Content-Length`. The form that the read named the stronger one; `put` and `remove` do not walk the sessions.
374. **The framing of a request** (the read, F4 and W7). A request with a body that the route does not take ends the connection after its answer, with each status: its body is not read as the next request (RFC 9112 6.3; with a proxy before the server, request smuggling). A close, not a read of the body that drops it: no new state, and a GET with a body has no meaning (RFC 9110 9.3.1). After the FIN of the remote, the whole requests in the input get their answers in order, then the close.
375. **The store first, then what is served** (the read, F5, W8, W9). `put` writes the store from one staging buffer of 60 KiB + 1, and copies into the page memory only when the store took it; `remove` calls the store first; a new page whose index the store refuses goes out again. A refused call leaves the page that is served as it was, and says so on the log. The start reads the store as hostile input: `web.page_index_entry` refuses an entry with no end, a name that breaks the rules, a repeated name; a page over 60 KiB is refused; `/` escapes each name (`web.html_text_escape`). A page that changes keeps its count of answers: it is the same name for the person.
376. **Bounds that are proven, and a log budget** (the read, W10, W11, W12). The held commands of `server` have the bound `PLACE_COUNT + RELEASE_WAITING_CAPACITY`: a Send or a Receive names one place, a place is in one command at most, and a Release is held only while fewer than `RELEASE_WAITING_CAPACITY` are; passing the bound stops the program (a check, not a loss). `fetch` has the bound `PLACE_COUNT`. The lines of the ends of connections have 50 in one second, and one line counts the others. An admitted remote serves again after more than 120 s with no segment: twice `RTO_MOST`, the longest gap between two probes of a zero window.
377. **The forms of a request that a server must take** (the read, notes). The absolute form of a target (RFC 9112 3.2.2: MUST): the scheme `http` or `https` and the authority go, the path stays. HTTP/1.2 is read as HTTP/1.1 (RFC 9110 2.5). Two empty lines before the request line are ignored (RFC 9112 2.2), a third is a bad request line. HTTP/1.0 with keep-alive gets `Connection: keep-alive`. Each answer says `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. The origin rule: the pages of the store, `/` and `/counts.json` share one origin, and a page of the store runs script on it by design: the origin holds no secret, no cookie and no authority (the counts are public, and a change of a page needs the console). The search for the end of a head starts where the last one stopped.
378. **No check depends on the load of the host** (the coordinator, 2026-11-11: the flake of `master` failed at a load of 28 on the 408 after half a head). The timers of `server` count on the clock of the machine, which a loaded host slows. A wait of the tool for an answer has a bound of 120 s (`HTTP_HOST_WAIT_LIMIT`), a peer case 180 s (`CASE_TIME_LIMIT`): bounds that end a test that hangs, not measures. The case of the page that changes waits for the step `Peer_Result`, not for a time. Changed: the HTTP case "errors" (408), "body-not-taken" and "requests-then-fin" (5 s before), `forward_fetch` (20 s before), each peer case (60 s before), and "page changed during its answer" (no time at all). Looked at and kept: the waits of the log (`STEP_TIMEOUT`, 20 s): each one that follows a timer of the server comes after a peer case or an HTTP case that already waited for that event; `slow`, `leak`, "zero window" and the FIN-WAIT-2 timer of the library compare no time.

347. **The fall of the wake rate of EEVDF has no cause in the corrections of choice 284** (part A of the next step after the merge). Choice 285 measured 114 643 wakes in one second for two threads that wake each other under EEVDF, against 313 601 to 332 954 in the three runs of choice 282; each was one run of the full machine test, on two different trees of `master` (the services of the call from the host and the desktop came between) and on a loaded host. The new command `kerneltool measure scheduler N` runs `scheduler` N times on a machine with only the desktop. With the kernel of this step (5 runs, load average of the host 2 to 5): round robin 397 to 400, fair 251 554 to 342 127, EEVDF 127 559 to 178 706 wakes in one second. With the kernel before the corrections (3 runs, the same program and tool): round robin 399 to 400, fair 205 561 to 272 386, EEVDF 147 666 to 173 462. The two ranges of EEVDF overlap: no correction (the hint, the limit, the early return, the check of a record, the move of `strategy_start`, the measure of a preemption) lowers it, so the step did not remove them one at a time. In these runs almost each turn that a wake ends is on the processor of the waker (EEVDF: 50 211 to 71 034 in the 400 ms of the case, by an interrupt 70 to 95): the two threads and a spinner share one processor; the limit of choice 284 does not apply (one program). EEVDF stays below the fair strategy because its wake path reads the queue three times (the place of the woken thread, the preemption, the lag at the wait) and its pick three times, where the fair strategy compares two numbers. The numbers of the full machine test depend on which programs share the processor of the two threads; the test asserts no speed, only that EEVDF wakes more often than round robin in the same run (more than 100 000 against 400 here). The worst time of one `Strategy_Set` was 3.9 ms and 20 ms in two runs of five (16 to 17 us in the others): the host took the processor of the machine; the middle was 1.7 to 1.8 us in each run.
348. **A frame cache for each processor** (`kernel/memory.odin`; the next form named in the gap of the frame lock, choice 221). Each processor keeps a stack of at most 256 free frames in its processor record, under its own cache lock: a page on first use takes its frame there, with no frame lock; an empty stack takes one frame and a batch of 128 in one section of the frame lock; the end of a memory object puts its frames on the stack of its processor (the frame lock is held there for the budget anyway); a full stack gives a batch of 128 back. A frame of a stack keeps its bit in `frame_bits` and is not in `free_frames`.
349. **A lock for each stack, not a drain by interrupt.** The brief offered two ways to take the frames of the other stacks back: an interrupt to each processor, as `processor_sync`, or a lock for each stack. A stack with no lock and interrupts off can be read and emptied only by its processor: a taker that holds the frame lock and waits for the other processors to empty their stacks needs them to take the frame lock, which it holds; and the report could not read the stacks. With a cache lock (kind Cache, after Frame in the lock order), a drain takes each cache lock in turn under the frame lock and always gets it, since no processor waits for the frame lock while it holds a cache lock. The cost: one uncontended lock pair for each page on first use and one for each end of a memory object; in the 4-locker measure the cache locks waited 3 to 349 times in 1.19 to 1.74 million takes (the waits are the drains and the reports of other processors).
350. **The sums stay exact.** `taken` of a stack counts the reserved pages that the stack served and that `reserved_pages` still counts. A take from a stack moves 1 from its frames to its `taken` in one section of its cache lock, so the sum frames + `taken` of a stack changes only under the frame lock (a batch, a give back, a fold of `taken`, a drain). `System_Report` holds the frame lock and reads each stack under its cache lock: free pages = `free_frames` + the frames of the stacks, reserved pages = `reserved_pages` - the `taken` of the stacks, exact while the other processors take. So the check "budgets N pages: all the free pages" of the console and the checks of `locks` stay as they were. The invariant: `free_frames` + the frames of the stacks = the pages of all budgets + `reserved_pages` - the `taken` of the stacks.
351. **No take fails while frames sit in a stack.** A take that cannot fail (a reserved page, a list page, a page table) and a contiguous object, with no free frame or no run in the frame table, drain all the stacks under the frame lock (`caches_drain`) and take again. The global exhaustion was not produced in the machine test (a budget never holds most of the memory there); the test build drains all the stacks at each 64th refill of a stack, so the drain runs on the same path while the other processors take and put: 2 to 2 315 drains of each stack in a measure.
352. **The check of the frames in the test build** (`TEST_BUILD_CHECKS_FRAME_SUMS`, as `TEST_BUILD_CHECKS_LOCK_ORDER`): at each report and each drain, under the frame lock and each cache lock, the free bits of `frame_bits` must be `free_frames`, each frame of a stack must have its bit set, and the invariant must hold; else the machine stops with code 3 and the numbers. It held in each run of this step.
353. **The zeros.** A frame gets its zeros when a page takes it, after the locks, as before (`region_frame`): a program never sees the old content of a frame (P20), and a frame on a stack is not zero. Zeroing at the put would hold the frame lock longer (the put is in the section of the end of a memory object) and would zero frames that a full stack gives back. The other order was not measured.
354. **The capacity, by measure** (`kerneltool measure`, `locks` with only the desktop, the 4-locker case of pages on first use; the host had a load of 6 to 15 during these runs): with no cache, the frame lock was taken 1 344 697 to 1 424 677 times and waited 170 677 to 270 701 times; with 16 frames, taken 143 256 to 180 885 times, waited 22 980 to 31 744; with 64, taken 50 330 to 58 208, waited 5 769 to 9 742; with 256, taken 10 179 to 13 449, waited 392 to 1 183. Chosen: 256 (2 KiB of the processor record; at most 1 MiB of free frames held by each processor, which a drain gives back). A locker faults 256 pages and frees them: with 256 the frames of one round stay on the stack. Pages on first use in one second, 1 / 2 / 4 lockers: 1 411 413 to 1 448 106 / 2 486 613 to 2 515 626 / 4 447 573 to 4 712 106 with no cache; 1 195 520 to 1 495 040 / 2 121 386 to 2 924 373 / 3 944 960 to 5 663 573 with 256. These rates moved with the load of the host by more than the change. Measured again after the rebase on the system copy and fill (choices 310 to 315; three runs each, the same tree with the kernel of `master` and with this one, load 7 to 23): pages on first use in one second, 1 / 2 / 4 lockers: before 1 830 400 to 2 303 146 / 3 081 386 to 3 758 933 / 4 791 466 to 6 493 866; after 2 174 293 to 2 345 813 / 3 933 866 to 4 410 026 / 7 278 080 to 8 057 173. Four lockers did 2.6 to 2.8 times one locker before, 3.3 to 3.4 times after. The frame lock in the 4-locker case: before taken 1 448 671 to 1 963 381 times and waited 303 382 to 405 521 times; after taken 18 071 to 20 635 times and waited 770 to 968 times; the cache locks waited 18 to 33 times in 2.2 to 2.4 million takes.
355. **Corrections after an independent read of the frame caches.** (F1) `region_create` of an object of more than 512 pages took its first list page in one section of the frame lock and the others in a second section; the budget paid for all of them in the first. Between the two, the free frames were above the pages of the budgets and the reserved pages by the list pages not yet taken, and a report or a drain on another processor stopped the machine. The stress case of `locks` reaches it: two lockers make and close objects of 600 pages (three list pages), two lockers fault pages, and `locks` reads `System_Report` with no pause; the lockers are other programs, so no program lock orders their entries (a first version in one program never reached the window: the program lock serialized the reader and the maker). Before the correction the machine stopped: "kernel: the frames do not add up: free bits 114808, free frames 114808, in stacks 0, marked 1, budgets 107883, reserved 6923, taken 0" (2 list pages). Now each list page is taken in the first section, and the zero fill of the list pages is after it; in each run since, 109 412 to 162 890 objects were made while `locks` read 67 901 to 113 363 reports, 0 wrong. (W2) The difference free pages - reserved pages is exact; the two alone are not values of one instant: the comment of `System_Report` and `ARCHITECTURE.md` say it, and the leak check of the console and the checks of `locks` compare only the difference. (N1) The `taken` of a processor that frees as much as it takes never folds: the report subtracts it; the comment says it. (N2) The comment of `frame_reserved_take` says what makes `processor_self` safe there.
356. **A test build and a measure build** (W1). The two checks of the kernel are now `TEST_BUILD_CHECKS_LOCK_ORDER` and `TEST_BUILD_CHECKS_FRAME_SUMS`, and the kernel tool passes both in each build: on for `check`, `test`, `screenshots`, `measure` and `run`, off for `measure without_checks` and `run without_checks`. Before, the defaults of the source were on in every build. The second check scans `frame_bits` under the frame lock at each report and each drain, and drains all the frame caches at each 64th refill. In the measure build no drain happened in any run (0 for each processor), so each drain of the test build came from the check. Measured with only the desktop (three runs of each, load of the host 6 to 8):

| `locks` | Test build | Measure build |
|---|---|---|
| Kernel entries in one second, 1 / 2 / 4 lockers (million) | 4.41-5.54 / 8.12-9.96 / 17.52-19.50 | 4.21-5.37 / 8.85-9.92 / 17.87-18.82 |
| Pages on first use in one second, 1 / 2 / 4 lockers (million) | 2.22-2.32 / 4.11-4.34 / 7.42-7.75 | 1.92-2.34 / 3.58-4.21 / 6.77-8.40 |
| Frame lock, 4 lockers: waits of takes | 1 003-1 923 of 20 442-25 002 | 578-1 194 of 16 004-19 812 |
| Stress: objects of 600 pages made in 500 ms | 109 412-119 053 | 119 576-162 890 |
| Stress: reports read in 500 ms | 67 901-71 107 | 85 106-113 363 |
| Drains of all the processors | 196-435 | 0 |

| `scheduler` | Test build | Measure build |
|---|---|---|
| Two threads that wake each other, wakes in one second: round robin / fair / EEVDF | 379-399 / 236 210-320 270 / 138 150-208 102 | 399-400 / 205 737-278 669 / 196 525-234 986 |
| Work of 8 spinners in one second (thousand million) | 8.8-11.6 | 11.3-14.7 |
| One `Strategy_Set`, in the middle | 2.0-2.6 us | 1.7-2.5 us |

The cost of the checks, as these runs show it: about 30 % of the reports and of the creations of large objects (a scan of 2 048 words of `frame_bits` at each report), and no difference in the pages on first use or the kernel entries that these runs can separate from the load of the host (the ranges overlap). The spinners did 25 to 30 % more work in the measure build in each run; their path enters the kernel only at the tick, so the part of the check in it is not shown, and the load of the host can be its cause. The stress cases ran in the two builds, with 0 wrong reports.
357. **What a drain in the measure build needs, and why the machine test does not make it.** The frame table becomes empty only when its frames are paid by budgets and in use, and the rest of the free frames are in the frame caches: the budgets reserve the frames, so a program can exhaust the frame table only with a budget that holds nearly all the free pages. In the machine test the root service keeps about 100 000 pages unpaid, and no program receives such a budget; a test of the real shortage needs a grant of most of the pages of the root service to one program, or a machine with very little memory where the desktop takes most of it. Not done in this step: the drain is tested on the same code path by the test build, while the other processors take and put.
358. **The zeros at the put, by argument** (point c of the read). A frame is zeroed when a page takes it (about 55 ns for 4 096 bytes with `rep stosb`, choice 312). Zeroing at the put costs the same for each frame that is used again, and more in total: a full stack gives frames back to the frame table, and those were zeroed for nothing; and the put is in the section of the frame lock of the end of a memory object, so 256 frames would hold the frame lock about 14 us longer for each object. It can make a first use about 55 ns faster (of about 700 ns), and it makes each end of an object slower under the one global lock. Not measured.
359. **The name of a static variable of a procedure: one cause that each build showed** (coordinator, 2026-11-11, after the full check of step 53 failed on "`kernel.module` is not the same bytes after a second build"; text corrected by 400). `kerneltool repeat kernel 40` under a host load of 31 gave: 0 of 39 modules different, but 39 of 39 LLVM IR files and 39 of 39 objects different. Two IR files of the same source differed in ONE line: the name `runtime::default_random_generator_proc-.state-N`, where N changed at each build (7400, 7582, 8185, 8437 in four builds; the same with the address layout of the compiler fixed by `setarch -R`). The name goes into the object as a symbol and, with `--data-sections`, as a section name. The tool then rewrote the IR before `llc` (`static_variable_names_make_stable`): N became the order of the first use of the name in the file. After it, `repeat kernel 6` gave 0 of 5 IR files and 0 of 5 objects different. **What this proved**: for 6 builds of one module, the rewrite made the objects equal. **What it did not prove**: that the objects of every module were equal from then on (`repeat host 30` gave 1 of 29 IR files, objects and modules different, with the rewrite), or that the name was the cause of a differing module (it was not: the name is not in the linked file). N is `Entity.id` (`global_entity_id`, src/entity.cpp; the name is made in `lb_build_static_variables`, src/llvm_backend_stmt.cpp), and it changed because two threads created entities (400). **Removed by 400**: with one thread N is the same at each build (6 builds of `console`: `state-8142` each time), and the rewrite hid the cause.
360. **One pointer theme file for a whole theme, read in place** (`contract/pointer_pictures`, `docs/POINTERS.md`, section 3; request of the owner, 2026-11-11: "notre propre format de stockage pour les curseurs [...] format texte [...] ou même binaire [...] je te laisse choisir"). Binary for the run time, text for the source (362): a header of 512 bytes with the name, the license and the attribution, a table of shapes, a table of pictures, the pixels. No parse at run time: one function `validate` checks each offset, count, text and pixel, and the tool and the compositor run the same function; 6 host tests damage each field (44 damages) and cut the file at each length. The pixels are 0xAARRGGBB with premultiplied alpha: the form of an Xcursor file, and the form of a canvas (0x00RRGGBB) plus the alpha in the high byte, so the blend takes each pixel with no conversion; straight alpha needs three more multiplications for each pixel. Two shapes can name the same pictures (Posy's `grab` and `grabbing` are one file). Not chosen: hyprcursor as the run-time format (a zip of PNG or SVG pictures with hyprlang manifests: a zip reader, an inflate, a PNG decoder, an SVG rasterizer and a parser in the compositor, for a gain in size that does not matter here); the owner named it during the step (`docs/POINTERS.md`, section 1).
361. **Fifteen pointer shapes, by name** (`Pointer_Shape`): `arrow` (0, the default of a zero record), `text`, `pointing_hand`, `move`, `grab`, `grabbing`, `wait`, `busy_with_arrow`, `forbidden`, `help`, `crosshair`, and the four resizes. Each has a CSS name, the source in the manifest of an Xcursor theme. The compositor uses `arrow`, `move`, `pointing_hand` and `text` from what it knows; the other shapes are for the request of a program. The CSS names with no use in the system are not taken; a new shape is a new number at the end.
362. **The pointer manifest: one text grammar for two sources** (`docs/POINTERS.md`, section 4, in 15 lines): a statement on each line (`theme`, `license`, `attribution`, `sizes`, `shape`, `frame`, and the lines of a grid after a `|`); a shape takes the first source file that exists in the Xcursor folder, through its links (8 at most, never out of the folder), or a grid of squares. Adjustments: a hot spot in pixels of the smallest size, which the tool scales for the other sizes, and the sizes of one shape. Each error gives the file, the line, the column, what was expected and what was found (23 host tests compare the sentences word for word). No scale of pixels: Posy's Cursor has the sizes 32 to 128, the tool keeps sizes that the file has and refuses another one with the list of the sizes of the file. The idea of the `meta.hl` of hyprcursor is the same (sizes, names, frames, hot spot); the hot spot here is in pixels, not a fraction of the size.
363. **The theme of the system**: 15 grids of squares in `resources/pointers/system.manifest`, under the license of the repository, in the style of the arrow of `DESIGN.md` (black at the edge, white inside), at the sizes 24 (squares of 2 pixels) and 48 (4 pixels); `wait` (an hourglass whose sand falls) and `busy_with_arrow` (the arrow with a turning bar) have 4 frames of 250 ms. It is always in the boot image: the system with no flake input has a whole theme.
364. **The theme of the owner is a build input, and the default when it is there** (owner, 2026-11-11, `HANDOFF.md` item 33). Its license is CC-BY-NC-4.0 (verified on 2026-11-11 in the meta of nixpkgs, the README and the `LICENSE` of `simtrami/posy-improved-cursor-linux`, and the page of Michiel de Boer: `docs/POINTERS.md`, section 6). nixpkgs marks that license unfree and not redistributable: `flake.nix` allows this one package by its name (`allowUnfreePredicate`), and gives its folder as `POINTER_THEME_SOURCE`. The tool keeps 32 and 64, and 32 only for the two shapes of 77 frames (1.26 MiB more at 64). The theme file carries the license and the attribution (who made it, the port, the license with its address, the sizes kept, no pixel changed), and the program `pointers` shows them.
365. **Resources of the boot image** (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`, U2b, the one description): a second table of `Image_Entry` after the modules (`Image_Header.resource_count`, which was a reserved zero), so that no reader of modules takes a resource for a module. The identity of a resource is the hash of its bytes (P11). No program reads the table by itself (P8): the root service finds a resource by its name, checks its hash (1.4 to 1.8 ms for 883 008 bytes in the loaded runs) and gives it as a read-only mapping (`resource_mapping`, `programs/root/resources.odin`); a program with the grant `Image` reads the table with `system.image_resources`. The choice of the theme is one function of the root service (`pointer_theme_choice`): a later setting of the person changes it there. The branch of the font uses the same mechanism for a glyph cache.
366. **The pointer in the compositor** (`programs/compositor/pointer_picture.odin`; 17 lines added to `main.odin` and 38 removed: an import, the field `pointer_shape` of a client, the call of `pointer_theme_open`, the wake of an animation in the deadline of the wait, `pointer_update` once in each turn of the loop, the case `Pointer_Shape` of a client command, `pointer_draw` and `pointer_frame_end`; removed: the arrow of squares and its constants, which moved to the new file as the arrow with no theme, and the two lines of damage of `pointer_moved`). It asks the nominal size 24: the smallest size that is at least 24 (32 for Posy, 24 for the system). The place of the pointer is unchanged (`compositor.pointer()` gives the same x and y); the picture is drawn with its hot spot there. `pointer_update` runs after each change of what is under the pointer, chooses the shape and the frame, and damages the old and the new rectangle of the picture only when the picture or its place changed. An animated shape wakes the compositor at the time of its next frame, and only while it shows. With no theme file, or a file that is not valid, the compositor draws the arrow of squares of before and writes why. `compositor.pointer()` has the field `shape`: the name of the shape under the pointer.
367. **The blend, written from nothing** (`graphics.blend_premultiplied_alpha`): red and blue in one 32-bit word, green alone, the division by 255 exact with `t = product + 128; (t + t / 256) / 256`; a pixel with alpha 0 is skipped, 255 copied; cut to the canvas. Host tests: each alpha, each premultiplied source and each destination of a channel (8.4 million cases), and a picture at each place around a canvas. Measured in the loaded runs: the arrow of Posy, 32x32 with 860 of 1024 pixels transparent, 824 to 1008 ns, against 151 to 153 ns for a copy of 32x32; the pointer in a frame, the mean of 256 frames, 1926 to 2040 ns.
368. **The request of a program** (`display.pointer_shape_set`, the display command `Pointer_Shape`): one shape for each surface, which the compositor shows only while the pointer is over that surface and the surface is the one in front at that place (P20: never over another program, the bar or a frame; the same rule as the serial of `wl_pointer.enter` in the cursor shape protocol of Wayland). A number out of the enum is ignored. The terminal asks for `text`; `pointers` asks for the shape of the tile under the pointer.
369. **The program `pointers`** (grant `Image`): each pointer theme of the resource table, its hash checked against its entry and its file validated (2.3 ms for Posy, 0.4 ms for the system, loaded), a tile for each shape with each size on a track, two amber lines through the hot spot, its frames, and the theme with its license, hash and attribution; two buttons change the theme. The window is built with the interface package; the pictures are blended into the canvas inside the tiles after the draw table, because the package has no record of a picture of pixels. The window of Posy drawn in 886 us.
370. **The step `Point` of the machine test**: it moves the pointer with no press, confirms its place, and reads the field `shape` of `compositor.pointer()` until it is the expected name (the tool reads a Text field of a record: `record_text_field`). With `inside_window` the place is from the corner of a window; a negative x is from its right side.
371. **The log keeps 2047 records** (`LOG_PAGES` 128 in `contract/operation`, 512 KiB of the kernel). The main machine wrote about 1024 records before the step that starts `log` and checks "0 lost": the first full check of this step failed there with "7 lost", from the 6 new lines of the pointer. The edge stays, at twice the place.
390. **The default font is a vector font, on the grid of cells** (owner, 2026-11-11, HANDOFF item 33; `docs/FONTS.md`). The font is monospace and its advance maps to exactly 8 pixels, the same scale on both axes: every layout in cells holds, no window changes its size, and `CELL_WIDTH`, `CELL_HEIGHT` and `text_width` do not change. The scales 2 and 3 are drawn from the outline at 16 x 32 and 24 x 48. A glyph is cut to its cell, so that a cell never draws into its neighbour (the terminal, the damage rectangles of the interface package). The baseline centers the ink of the characters 33 to 126 in the cell: row 12 at scale 1 for JetBrains Mono, the row of Spleen.
391. **A grid fit of the vertical metrics, no hinting instructions.** The baseline, the x-height and the cap height go to whole pixels (piecewise linear in the vertical, `vector_font.place_y`); the horizontal positions keep their thirds of a pixel. The subsets carry no instructions. The picture `build/fonts/candidates.png` has the default with and without it.
392. **Subpixel bands with the filter of five taps** (8, 77, 86, 77, 8 of 256): measured on a stem of one pixel, a fringe of 162 against 170 for three taps and 255 for none. Each pixel of the cache is one word: the three filtered coverages and the coverage of the whole pixel for grayscale. The order of the stripes is a setting: `rgb`, `bgr` (a swap at the blend, exact), `none` (grayscale).
393. **The blend in linear light with a gamma of 2, in integers** (`graphics.blend_coverage_row`): at most 8.9 levels from the curve of sRGB for the colors of the rules, against 54.9 levels for a blend with no gamma; a table of 4096 square roots that each program makes once, indexed by a shift.
394. **A rasterizer in fixed point** (24.8 subunits, `i32` accumulations; the overflow bound is 8192 segments for each glyph; cuts that two pieces share): the sum of the coverage of a closed outline is its area, exact for edges along the axes and at 45 degrees, within 0.000029 of the area for others. It replaces the rasterizer in floating point of step 51, and with it the write outside a row for a vertical edge on the right side of the last column (host test `the_last_column`).
