395. **The glyph cache is built at build time and is a resource of the boot image**, against a cache in each program (3.7 to 4.0 ms and 390 624 bytes for each program) and a font service (one more program, the font file at run time). It is a function of the font file (its BLAKE3 in the header) and of `RENDERER_VERSION` (P11). The root service checks its hash once (796 to 966 us) and maps it read-only into each program with a surface, each terminal and the compositor (P6, P20). It is a second resource of the mechanism of step 55 (choice 365), given by the same `resource_mapping`.
396. **JetBrains Mono is the default font, as an ASCII subset** among six candidates with `glyf` outlines (DejaVu Sans Mono, IBM Plex Mono renamed "IBM Mono" for its Reserved Font Name, Fira Mono, Commit Mono, Iosevka); Source Code Pro has CFF outlines only. `kerneltool vector_fonts` makes the subsets (14 to 21 KB each, from 156 KB to 50 MB) from the build inputs of the development shell; they are in `resources/fonts` with their licenses, so a build needs no build input. One line changes the default.
397. **The settings page** (`contract/settings`): one page that the root service creates, the program `settings` writes, and each program with a surface reads at a fixed address given in the value 13 of its context. A change wakes the compositor, which sends the display event `Settings_Changed` to each window; `support/display` gives the new font to `support/graphics` when the program reads the event, and the interface package draws the whole window. All zero is the default. The program `settings` serves `set` and `get` with error records.
398. **Programs did not change for the font.** The five private copies of large text read the bitmap of Spleen directly; each now calls `graphics.text_scaled`. The boot display swaps the stripes on a screen with red in the low byte.
399. **The text from the glyph cache, measured**: one glyph 309 ns against 35 ns for Spleen in the machine (333 to 362 ns against 33 to 44 ns on the host), after a loop for each stripe order, no bounds check after the one validation of the cache, and the squares of the pixel under kept; 921 ns before these. `flood` wrote 4 302 808 lines in one second against 3 925 316 on `master` in the same hour, the terminal drew 62 pictures in one second in both; the host load was 15 to 37 (above 8: not valid measures of speed by the rule).
403. **A random generator in each program, a seed from its starter** (`docs/RANDOMNESS.md`; the coordinator preferred this form to a kernel operation or a service call for each draw). Each program has a ChaCha20 generator with fast key erasure, a record in its own memory; a draw makes no kernel entry and no call. Its seed is 32 bytes in the context values 9 to 12 (`op.CONTEXT_VALUE_SEED`), which its starter writes from its own stream; the program erases them after it reads them. `system.program_start` refuses a seed of zeros. The root service has no starter: it gathers the seed of the system at its start. A child has no secret from its starter, which already chooses its code and its grants.
404. **The seed of the system claims no more than its sources.** BLAKE3 of 8 values of `rdseed`, 8 of `rdrand` and 2048 deltas of the cycle counter, each with its length. The strength is that of the strongest source that gave all its values: entropy (`rdseed`), processor (`rdrand`), or weak (the cycle counter, counted as 0 bits). A value with the carry clear, all ones, or equal to one before it is refused. The root service writes the report on the log, and gives its facts (never the seed) to the program `randomness`. With no `rdrand` and no `rdseed`, the system starts and says "weak".
405. **The inline asm of `rdseed` and `rdrand` has `#side_effects`.** Without it, LLVM took the instruction as a pure function: in a loop it kept one value, and the check of repeated values refused 255 of 256 (`kerneltool test tcp` showed "rdrand present, 1 good, 255 refused"). `tcp.secret_make`, which read `rdrand` in a loop with the same form, is gone: `server` and `fetch` take their secret from their generator.
406. **The cryptography is code of Odin's `core:crypto`, taken into the repository** (owner, 2026-11-11: "don't write cryptography from nothing, but maybe incorporate existing stuff and adjust it/provide wrappers functions/compat, so that they use OUR kernel primitives (tables, modules, shit like that)"; `docs/CRYPTOGRAPHY.md`). The files keep their arithmetic and their constant-time code. Only their imports change, to the copies and to a compatibility layer. `kerneltool cryptography-origin` proves it for each file of `support/cryptography/origin.manifest`: the origin in the compiler of the flake has the BLAKE3 hash of the manifest, and the origin with the listed changes is the file, byte for byte. The zlib license of Odin and the BSD-1-Clause of fiat-crypto are beside the code; each file says in its first line that it is altered. `erase_secret_bytes` in `runtime/memory.asm` erases a secret: a call into an object of nasm, which the compiler cannot remove with no link-time optimization.

400. **One source gives one module: the Odin compiler runs on one thread, by a patch that the flake applies** (`tools/odin_compiler/thread_count_counts_the_main_thread.patch`, `flake.nix`; property 11; the open fault of the gaps). The cause, in the source of the compiler of the flake (tag `dev-2026-05`): `init_global_thread_pool` (src/main.cpp) makes `worker_count = thread_count` workers beside the main thread, so `-thread-count:1` gives two threads (counted: 2 threads in `/proc/PID/task` during a build). The worker steals the tasks of the main thread. In `check_collect_entities_all` (src/checker.cpp) each file is one task, and each task adds the entities of its file to the shared queue `info.entity_queue`. That queue gives the order of `info.entities` (`check_add_entities_from_queues`), and the checker walks the declarations in that order. That order changes the order of the instances of polymorphic procedures and of the types in the LLVM module, and the inlining of `-o:speed` changes with it. The entity counter (`global_entity_id`) changes too, and with it the name of choice 359. `-no-threaded-checker` changes only `check_procedure_bodies`. The parser, the collection of the entities, the exports and the dependency walks still use the pool. Measured with the stock compiler and the flags of the tool, the process pinned to one processor (`taskset`): `console` 3 distinct LLVM IR files in 60 builds (49, 10, 1), `host` 2 in 60 (56, 4). With no `-no-threaded-checker` and no rewrite: `console` 5 distinct modules in 25 builds (3, 19, 1, 1, 1) and 15 distinct IR files. The patch: `-thread-count:N` means N threads in all, the main thread and N-1 workers, as the backend of the compiler already counts (`lb_generate_code`). With one thread the main thread runs each task in `thread_pool_wait`, in one order. A thread that waits for a signal with no worker (`wait_signal_until_available`, the fields of an `#soa` type that a queued task completes) runs the tasks of its own queue: no other thread would run them. With workers that wait is unchanged. Measured with the patch, pinned to one processor: `console` and `host` 60 builds each, one IR file each, one thread in each compiler process. The patch changes two files of the compiler (28 lines added, 10 of them comments; 3 removed); the flake builds the compiler from source (about 5 minutes, one time). **The cost**: the Odin step of the 61 modules, the two compilers one after the other on each module: 39.2 s (stock, two threads) and 40.3 s (patched), then 32.9 s and 34.1 s, with the same processor time (36.5 s and 36.3 s, 32.4 s and 32.4 s); host load 17 to 28. **Refused**: (i) a flag or a variable of the stock compiler that gives one thread: none exists (`-thread-count` is the only one, and it gives two threads); (iii) `-o:none` IR from Odin, a sort of the IR by the tool, then `opt -O3`: it changes the code of each module and needs a canonical form of the IR that the tool must keep correct, and the cause stays in the compiler. **For the upstream project** (not sent: the owner decides): the patch file is the text of an issue and of a change; its first lines state the fault, the lines of the source and the measures. **Not tested**: the wait of an `#soa` type with no worker. The case needs a struct that is not complete when its `#soa` type is made; the small programs tried did not reach it, and two of them crash the stock compiler too.

401. **The check and `repeat` compare each output of a build, keep each distinct result, and `repeat all` builds everything** (`tools/kerneltool/repeat.odin`). The check of the second build compares, for each module, the module, its LLVM IR and its object, the pointer theme files, and the boot program and its object (before: the module only; choice 359 showed that 39 of 39 objects can differ while 0 of 39 modules differ). `repeat <module> <count>` counts the distinct results of each output and the builds of each ("3 distinct in 30 builds: 27, 2, 1"); before, it compared each build with build 1 only, so one rare first build made "29 of 29 builds differ". `repeat all <count>` builds every module and the boot program `count` times and prints a table: the module, the builds, the distinct modules, LLVM IR files and objects. Each distinct result stays as a file in `build/different_builds/<module>/<file>.variant_N` (a folder that no build resets: the build of a module resets its folder of objects). The tool prints the first differing line of an IR file, the part of a module file, or the first differing byte of another file.

402. **The rewrite of the names of static variables (choice 359) and `-no-threaded-checker` (choice 189) are removed.** With one thread, the name of a static variable is the same at each build, and the rewrite only hid the cause. `-no-threaded-checker` has no effect when the pool has one thread (`check_procedure_bodies` takes its sequential path when `threads.count` is 1). The tool `kerneltool` itself is compiled by `odin run` with the default count of threads (16 on this host). It is not a module, and property 11 does not cover it. Measured with the patched compiler and one output name: 6 builds of the tool with the default count of threads gave 6 distinct files, and 4 builds with `-thread-count:1` gave 1. The flake keeps the default count for the tool: a faster start of each command, and no property needs the same bytes.

407. **The compositor draws its screen as one draw table** (`screen_build`): for each window its frame records and its surface as a `Picture` record, then the bar and the menus as cards of the package. The table is built once for each frame and executed in each damage area; the damage of the compositor stays its own, because a window that moves moves its surface. The frame of 800 x 600 costs 8.9 us as 9 records against 8.5 us by hand (one measure run under load: an indication).
408. **The boot display uses the card of the package**, in the UEFI loader too: the core files of the package make no kernel call. A fact longer than its card is cut after its last whole word.
409. **`paint`, `plasma`, `temple` show their pixels as `Picture` records**; a program that is one picture calls `interface.show_picture`. `paint` raises the generation of its paper at each change, including the changes of its exports.
410. **The text grid record** (`Text_Grid`, draw record version 3): the cells stay in the memory of the program, each table before keeps a copy, the difference damages the span of changed cells in each row. The console needs nothing more: it writes records to its terminal.
411. **With two pictures a frame presents its difference with the picture on the screen too** (`damage_of_two_pictures`). A frame equal to its own picture can differ from the screen; the terminal showed rows of two frames before this rule. A host simulation of two pictures and a screen holds it.
412. **Window places on the grid of 8**: new windows start at 64,56 and step by 40,32; a moved window snaps its corner to the nearest place of the grid. The scenarios move windows with steps `Move`, and the tool reads the places from `compositor.windows()` once it has the grant person.
413. **`kerneltool measure windows`**: a measure build (`-define:MEASURE_RUN=true`) starts each converted window alone and prints the time of its first picture and the means of 3 and 10, with the load of the host; above a load of 8 the table says that it is not valid. The lines of the measure are never in a normal build: the log ring of the main scenario has little room.
414. **Parallel module builds: one process of the tool for each module, in levels of imports** (`tools/kerneltool/parallel_build.odin`; owner, 2026-11-11, HANDOFF item 43). `kerneltool build` runs the command `module <name> with_checks|without_checks measure_run|usual_run` of the tool itself for each module of the table, at most one process for each processor at once, or `build <processes>`. Each process runs one compiler on one thread (choice 400), so one source still gives one module. A module needs the files of its imports only at its conversion, so the modules are built in levels: level 0 has no import, and a module is one level after the highest level of its imports (today 2 levels: `test_caller`, `agenda`, `lonely` and `imports` are in level 1). The tool keeps the standard output and the standard error of each process, and prints them whole, in the order of the table, after the level. After a failure no new process starts; the tool names each module that failed and gives the count of the modules that did not start. The boot images are assembled after the modules, in the order of the table. Each module writes only in its own folder, in `<name>.module`, and in `<name>.ll` in the folder of the repository (the compiler writes its IR where it runs); the tool refuses a table with two equal names. Separate processes, not threads in the tool: the build of a module uses global state of the tool (the temporary memory, `test_build_checks`), and a process gives its whole output and its own exit code. The check "the same bytes after a second build" and `repeat all` use the same parallel build (`repeat all <count> <processes>`); `repeat <module>` builds one module in its own process, as before. **Measured** (`kerneltool build`, the whole command with the boot programs, the two forms in alternation, host load 20 to 46, so above the limit of 8 for a valid measure): `build 1` 171 s and 99 s, `build` with 16 processes 82 s and 58 s. A parallel build of all modules gave the same 63 modules and boot program as the sequential build (compared byte for byte). A broken source of `life` gave its whole error, "the build of life failed", "54 of 63 modules did not start, because a module failed", and exit 1. **Not run by this agent** (the rule of 2026-11-11 for subagents: the coordinator builds and tests): `repeat all 100` with the parallel build, the full check, the flake.
415. **The compiler is the release dev-2026-11a of Odin, pinned by the flake** (`flake.nix`; owner, 2026-11-11, HANDOFF item 56). The flake fetches the release by its tag and its hash and builds it with LLVM 18, the compiler and its `base` and `core` libraries only. What the six releases since May changed for this system: `-out` of an LLVM IR build is a folder (the tool gives an empty folder and takes its one file); the inline assembly by a string is gone, so `rdrand` and `rdseed` are `asm` templates with the carry flag as a result (`support/random/entropy.odin`); the analysis of atomic accesses found reads by `volatile_load` of words that other code writes atomically, in the ring, the wait and three programs: they are atomic loads now; `odin run` gives a program only the name of its file, so the parallel build starts the file of the process; a stricter conversion in `fetch`; `core:compress` in the tool. The patch of the thread count is one line: the wait that its second part corrected has no caller left. The files of ChaCha20 are taken again from the new release. `kerneltool check`: exit 0, 835 checks, 64 modules the same bytes after a second build.
416. **The compiler has no patch and uses all its threads** (`flake.nix`, `tools/kerneltool/modules.odin`; owner, 2026-11-11; `docs/REPRODUCIBLE_BUILD.md`, section 7). With the release dev-2026-11a, the output of the compiler does not depend on the count of its threads: `kerneltool repeat all 20` with the stock compiler and no `-thread-count` gave 1 distinct module, LLVM IR and object for each of 64 modules and the boot program. The patch of choice 400 and the option `-thread-count:1` are removed. The wider patch of the compiler for a deterministic order with several threads, the last item of the list of the owner, is not needed: the compiler has that property now.
417. **Two parameter types, one direction for each parameter** (owner, 2026-11-11: "ok bah on le fait alors ?"; `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "Tables and bytes as parameters"). A Table parameter (the type 5, a result before) and Bytes (6), each with a direction: Read (0), Change (1), Append (2). Read is 0: the least power is the zero. Append covers fill (from a count of 0) and append (the draw table of the interface, which each element call extends): the callee writes after the count, reads none before it, and gives the count of the full answer, larger than the capacity when it did not fit, as a Text result does. The Table result stays as it is: it is the case "Append from 0 in the last place, whose count is the integer result". Refused: tables of tables, a table in a record, an address in a record, a Handle in a record of a table, a direction on another type, a capacity rule in the description. A record can name bytes of another parameter by an offset: data, not an address; the export checks it (the error kind `Out_Of_Range` of `master`, whose `found` is now the number of the record that names the bytes).
418. **The table place**: a Table or Bytes parameter takes one register, the address of `{address, count, capacity}` (24 bytes, three u64) in memory of the caller. Three u64 is the same layout in C, Odin, Rust and Zig; an Odin slice `{data, len}` has no capacity. No record size travels with it: an import names the exact module by hash, and the check of a replacement compares the record and the direction of each parameter (`Difference.Parameter_Direction`). The rule of the table directory for a shorter record is for the tables of a file, not for parameters. In Odin the type is the direction: `system.Table_To_Read(Reading)`, `Table_To_Change`, `Table_To_Append`, `Bytes_To_Read`, `Bytes_To_Change`, `Bytes_To_Append`.
419. **The format grows by its own rule**: `Function.parameter_directions` (6 bytes, and 2 reserved) at the end of the Functions record, 96 to 104 bytes. A reader of a file with 96-byte records reads Read for each parameter. No version and no feature bit: each module is built with the system (owner: no backward compatibility), and a module of before still loads. Integer fields of 1, 2, 4 and 8 bytes: `Field.detail` is the size of an Integer (0 for 8, the fields of before), `Field.unsigned` takes a reserved byte; an Integer is at a multiple of its size. `module.field_integer` reads one; the console, the tool and the manual read fields with it.
420. **The checks of a description are where a reader uses it**, as before: the loader does not read descriptions. `module.record_type` checks the size and the alignment of each Integer field; `isolated.record_size` refuses a direction out of the enumeration or on another type, a Table whose record is not valid or has a Handle, Bytes with a detail; `information.check` gives the problem `Direction` (12) with its byte.
421. **The isolated call carries a field of 32 bytes** `{start, count, capacity, memory}`. With memory 0 the caller copies the records of a Read or Change table into the call region, and reserves the free records of an Append table (the records before the count are not in the call: record `count` of the place of the receiver is the first reserved byte). The receiver copies the argument record once, checks each field (a start at a multiple of 8, the records inside the region with no overflow, count <= capacity for Append) and gives the export a table place in its own memory. After the export it writes each Append count back into the field; the caller copies back the records 0 ..< count of a Change table and count ..< min(new count, capacity) of an Append table, never at or after the capacity, and refuses a count smaller than the count on entry. 10 host tests in `contract/isolated/tables_test.odin`, among them a guard pattern after the capacity with a callee that writes the whole call region, 100 000 random fields, and each hostile field.
422. **A table in a memory object** (form iii): the field names a lent memory object; the rights of the lent copy are the direction (Read; Read and Write), and the receiver proves them as each lent handle (a Change table lent with Read only gives Bad_Handle: a case of `tables`). The receiver maps only the pages of the records, at `isolated.TABLE_ADDRESS + n * 16 GiB`, the same addresses at each call: with an address that the kernel chooses, each call took a new range and new page tables, and the call host refused the 61st call (found by the measure). `system.call_isolated` lends the memory object only when the records do not fit in the call region: the call region costs less at each size that fits.
423. **One caller side for the three ways** (`support/system/calls.odin`): `call_direct` (the registers of the call form over table places on the memory of the arguments, no copy), `call_isolated` (lend, encode, the wait, the copy back, the revocation; it gives the handles of the caller back in the arguments after the call), `call_host_start` (the start of a call host from the boot image, as the console does it) and `service_connection`. The console keeps its own call host code; moving it to `call_host_start` is a next step.
424. **`sample` has an entry**: the same module is a library (`load`), a call host (`isolate`) and a service that the root service starts on demand (`connect("sample")`, the grant Serve). Its exports: `readings` (a Table result), `sum_values` (Read), `scale_values` (Change), `append_readings` (Append), `sum_words` (a table whose records name bytes of a Bytes(Read) parameter, with an error record `Out_Of_Range` for a word past the end), `upper_case` (Bytes(Change)). The exports that are wrong on purpose (`append_past_capacity`, an Append export that writes past its capacity, and `keep`, `kept`, `hold`, `look` of the tests of a lent handle) are in the module `hostile_exports`, with no entry: no service holds them (finding B1 of the hostile read: the service `sample` held `append_past_capacity`, and `remote` reached it, so a host of the network made the service write past its call region). `remote` does not reach `sample`.
425. **The console**: a Table value is an argument where the description takes a Table of an equal record (`module.records_equal`: `columns` gives another record, which is refused with a sentence), with the direction Read or Change; a Text is an argument of Bytes(Read). A Change table changes the memory of the value: a variable holds the changed rows. An export with an Append table or Bytes(Change) or Bytes(Append) is not a command of the console: the language has no second result. One text of a signature in the console, the manual and the errors: `sum_words(words: Table(Word, Change), text: Bytes(Read)) -> Integer`.
426. **`kerneltool call`**: a table argument is a literal of rows, each row the values of its fields in order, `[(1, "a"), (2, "b")]`; Bytes(Read) take a text. A Change or Append table is refused: the answer of `remote` carries one result place. The test calls `monitor.counts_named` (a Table(Count_Name, Read): many counts in one call); `remote` does not reach `sample`.
427. **The interface package as a module for one program** (`modules/interface_module`, `programs/interface_through_module`; `INTERFACE.md`, section 7, "The test of the claim"). Eight exports call the package with no line of the package changed. An export makes a frame of the package over the two tables of the caller for one call; the interface state and the input point to two empty records of the module, which these elements never write. The first form zeroed 1.5 KiB at each call: 26 ns for each element call, 10 ns after the change.
428. **Measured** (`MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "Measures"; the load of the host 32 to 48: not valid by the limit of 8, the numbers of that run): `sum_values`, one call, 64 / 1024 / 65536 records of 40 bytes: direct 12 / 250 / 26 284 ns; call host, call region 6 671 / 8 787 ns / does not fit; call host, memory object 12 500 / 13 146 / 76 422 ns; service, call region 3 327 / 8 217 ns / does not fit. One frame of 31 draw records in 25 element calls: 523 ns through `interface_module`, 283 ns with the package compiled in. The code: `interface_module` 14 980 bytes; the functions of the package that the compiler kept in `clock` 8 865 bytes, in `interface_elements` 24 948 bytes. The full check after the rebase on step 55 (the load 21 to 23; the package had changed under the module, with no change to the module): direct 15 / 348 / 26 051 ns; call host, call region 7 712 / 10 852 ns; call host, memory object 17 868 / 19 338 / 110 615 ns; service, call region 3 102 / 9 010 ns; one frame 741 ns through the module, 370 ns compiled in; `interface_module` 24 576 bytes of code pages.
429. **The corrections of the hostile read** (2026-11-11, `review_tables.md`). F1: an Append place with a count near 2^64 passed `decode` in the call region (`start - count` wrapped), and `system.bytes_append` added the count and an index with a wrap: 11 bytes past the call region. `decode` now refuses a count or a capacity over `TABLE_SPAN / size` in each form; `bytes_append` compares the index with the free bytes, and its count, as the count of `table_append`, stops at the largest number. Tests: `decode_refuses_counts_near_the_end_of_the_numbers` (contract/isolated), `bytes_append_near_the_end_of_the_numbers` (support/system, now in the host tests of `kerneltool check`); both failed before. F2: `interface_module.execute_in_rectangles` checked `stride * height * 4` with a product that wraps (a stride of 2^62 + 1): it now compares the stride with the pixels of one line that the bytes hold. W4: a text record that names bytes past its text table stopped the module in `damage_by_difference`, and a capacity of 2^63 was a negative int: the module refuses a table over 2^40 records or bytes with -1 (the check of each text range moved into `support/interface` in 429). Tests: `canvas_shape_that_wraps_is_refused`, `hostile_records_are_refused` (modules/interface_module, now in the host tests of `kerneltool check`); both failed before. W2: `sample.sum_words` read the start of a word two times from the shared call region: another thread of the caller made a slice that stopped the service; it copies the record once with a volatile load (the rule "an export copies a record before it checks it" in `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`). Test: `a_record_that_changes_during_the_call` (modules/sample, a thread changes the record during 2 000 000 calls; it stopped on an Illegal_Instruction before). W5: `interface_through_module` used the count of an Append table as it came: it draws `min(count, capacity)` records, and the scenario builds one frame into a draw table of 16 places ("the frame did not fit, 17 records asked, 16 drawn"). Corrected again in 429: the count is the count of the full frame. W1: a service refuses a table in a memory object (`export_serve` takes `memory_objects_allowed`, true only in the call host): a caller that revoked the lent copy during the export stopped the service for each caller; case of `tables`: "a memory object to the service". W3: `decode` refuses two ranges of the call region that overlap when one is written; host test `decode_refuses_ranges_that_overlap`. W6: `system.call_direct` checks the description with `record_size`, refuses a result that is not an Integer (`Not_Done`; it gave the address of the error record where `readings` reads its buffer) and a table whose record is not valid; case of `tables`: "call_direct of a Table result". W7: `tables_return` checks each answer, then copies (a problem changes no count), with `Bad_Description`; `call_isolated` gives the parameter in the error record; host test `tables_return_changes_nothing_on_a_problem`. W8: each refusal of the receiver is an error record in the completion; host test `decode_refusal_is_an_error_record`, cases of `tables` with their sentences. N1: a record size is a multiple of its alignment (host test). N2: a table in a memory object reaches at most `TABLE_SPAN` less one page (host test). N3: a table names a memory object (case "a budget as the memory of a table": Handle_Kind). N4: a value that its Integer field does not hold is refused with a sentence in the console and in `kerneltool call` (`module.field_integer_fits`, host test; 300 in a `u8` became 44). N5, N7: in `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`. N6: `remote` zeroes its call region after the arguments before each call: a request reads no bytes of a call before it (no test: the tool has no request that names stale bytes). N8: the wording, and a redundant check of `information.check`. Names of the review: `memory_handle`, `writable`, `count_offsets`, `lent_handle_add`, `is_table_or_bytes`, `lend_one_handle`, `mapping_address`, `records_in_region`, `reachable_records`, `count_given_by_callee`, `kept_records`, `draw_table_to_read_of`, `table_description_of`. The fixed address of a lent table stays a work-around of the kernel fault "a mapping with no chosen address never reuses a range", given to `work/scheduler`.
430. **The corrections of the second hostile read** (2026-11-11, `review_tables_second.md`). F-new: `interface_module` used a count over the capacity on entry as a mark that the contract refused; it worked in a direct call only, and an isolated call gave `No_Room` with a wrong reason. Decision of the coordinator: the count of an Append table is the count of the full answer across calls, and on entry it can be over the capacity (`MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "Did not fit, across calls"). `isolated.table_encode` and `table_decode` take it (the records in the region are `capacity - min(count, capacity)`; record `min(count, capacity)` is the first reserved byte), `tables_return` copies `min(count, capacity) ..< min(new count, capacity)`, `system.call_direct` takes it. `interface_module` builds each element into an element frame of its own on the stack (64 records, 4096 bytes of text; a larger element gives -1) and appends it to the tables of the caller with `system.table_append` and `system.bytes_append`: the counts of the full answer, also of the text table. Tests: `append_across_calls_is_the_same_in_each_way` (contract/isolated: three calls on a table of 4 places, the same counts 3, 6, 9 and the same records in a direct call and through encode, decode, counts_store and tables_return), `append_after_a_call_that_did_not_fit` (support/system), `counts_of_the_full_answer_across_calls` (modules/interface_module), the scenario line "records asked, 16 drawn, the count of the full frame". W-new: the check of the text range of each record moved from the module (a check in shared memory, then a second read in the package) into `support/interface` where it reads: `records_equal` gives "not equal" for a text range past its table, and `damage_by_difference` takes one volatile copy of each record; test `text_range_past_the_table_is_damaged`. Notes: (1) `export_serve` proves the lent handles and defers their close before the refusal of a memory object to a service; (2) `remote` zeroes its call region with one fill; (3) `Overlap` with the unit Region is an offset in the call region, and `Absent` names the row of a table argument in `found`, with their sentences; (4) a handle that fails the proof gives `Handle_Absent` with the unit `Lent_Handles` ("names no handle that the caller lent for this call"), or `Handle_Rights` with the rights asked and held.
431. **The real shortage of frames, in a machine of the test** (part A of the step after the frame caches). The frame table is empty only when the unspent pages of all the budgets and the reserved pages are fewer than the frames in the stacks (at most 256 for each processor): on the machine of the desktop the programs keep thousands of unspent pages, so it never happens there. A new machine of the test has a boot image with no input service (`build/boot_shortage.image`); the root service sees it and starts only the program `shortage`, with all its pages but 24, and the machine (Inspect, Stop). Four threads make objects and write each page (takes that cannot fail) until the budget pays no more; the last pages of the budget become one object that is not used. Measured (one run of each build, load of the host 26 to 43): 116 692 pages written in 2 163 ms with the checks of the test build, 23 drains; 116 694 in 2 857 ms in the measure build, 3 drains: each one a real drain, the measure build has no other. Then a new object of 1 page fails with `No_Budget` and the error record of kind Budget, while the report says that the budgets can pay 17 pages (those that the root service kept) and 192 pages are reserved: a take that can fail fails on the budget, before any frame; the frames that remain are the frames of other budgets. Everything is freed in 5 to 7 ms, and the free pages that budgets can pay are those of the start less 231 pages of page tables, which `Memory_Unmap` does not give back (the gap of choice 224) and which return when the program stops. The machine stops with the code 0. `kerneltool test shortage`, `kerneltool measure without_checks shortage`; the main test runs it after the machine with no compositor. No picture: the machine has no desktop.
432. **A program drives its own virtual memory** (owner, 2026-11-10: "a program drives its own virtual memory", "use paging and virtual memory as a design tool"; `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The virtual memory of a program"). An uncommitted object (`Memory_Create` with the new flag `Uncommitted`) pays only its frame list; its mapping holds a range of addresses with no memory behind it, in one mapping of the 128 of a program. `Memory_Commit` (operation 28) pays pages of a table of ranges, and with `Decommit` takes them back at once; `Memory_Protect` (operation 29) changes the protection of pages of a table of address ranges, with one shoot-down. Why two operations and not kinds of existing ones: no existing operation changes what a budget pays for some pages of an object that lives (`Memory_Create` makes one, `Memory_Map` and `Memory_Unmap` change addresses), and none changes the protection of some pages of a mapping that lives. Why one operation for commit and decommit: the two change the same three counts (the budget, the reserved pages, the frames) over the same table, in opposite directions. The kernel had 27 counted operations; it has 29.
433. **A table of ranges in one command.** `Memory_Commit` and `Memory_Protect` take at most 256 ranges in memory of the caller (P13, P14). The kernel ring already batches 32 commands for one entry, but each command takes the program lock again and checks its handle again, and a protection change would do one shoot-down for each; a table takes the lock once, checks once, and does one shoot-down. Measured: kernel entries for 1000 pages committed and used, with 1, 16 and 256 ranges in each call: 1000, 62 and 3, in each run (the first use of a page is a fault, not an entry).
434. **The state of a page is in its slot of the frame list.** A frame is aligned on 4096 bytes, so the low 12 bits of a slot hold: committed (for an uncommitted object), deny write, deny execute, no access. So the protection of a part of a mapping needs no new mapping (no split of the 128 mappings), and a page that gets its frame later gets the protection of its slot. Each reader masks the frame (`region_frame`). The cost: the protection of a part, and a decommit, need an object that only this program names, with one mapping (`Bad_State` with its error record otherwise): the slots belong to the object, and its entries must be in one page table. A contiguous object (device memory, large pages) has no frame list: its protection is of the whole mapping.
435. **Decommit in two steps.** First the entries of the pages are cleared and each processor that uses the page table forgets them (one `processor_sync`); then the frames go to the frame cache of the processor, the budget gets each page back. The program lock keeps the faults of the program out between the two. The content of a page committed again is zero: it gets a new frame (P20, ZII); the program checks it.
436. **Large pages for an object made `Large_Pages`**: contiguous, in one run aligned on 2 MiB (`frames_take_aligned`), a whole number of 512 pages (else `Bad_Operation` with its error record). A mapping whose address, first page and size are whole large pages uses one entry of the second level for each 2 MiB; another mapping of the same object uses pages of 4 KiB. The walk of the page table gives the entry of the second level for an address in a large page; the end of a mapping clears it; the free of the page tables of a program does not free a large page as a table; a table of the last level that a range left returns to the budget after a shoot-down when a large page takes its place. A protection change of a part of a large mapping is refused (the whole mapping changes). The kernel decides the size of a page from the object and the mapping: the program asks only for an object whose frames allow it.
437. **The fault ring is designed, not built** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The fault ring"). It needs a wait of a thread that no word ends, a record that names the faulting thread and its serial, and a rule for a thread that stops while it waits for its answer: three new states of a thread, which deserve their own step and their own review. A guard page that stops a program is built: `Memory_Map` and `Memory_Protect` take `No_Access`.
438. **The program `address_space`** (window through `support/interface`): an arena of 1 GiB that commits 3 x 4096 pages with 1, 16 and 256 ranges for each entry; a sparse table of 1 GiB with 1024 pages committed at random; the decommit of each, with the budget back at once; the protection of 64 pages of a mapping of 256, 200 calls, with 0, 1 and 3 other threads of the program running; a child program that writes down its stack into a guard page; 64 MiB of large pages read at random through a mapping of large pages and through one of 4 KiB; four refusals with their error records (a range outside the object, a commit over the budget, write and execute, large pages of 100 pages). The checks do not depend on the load of the host: the committed pages and the pages with a frame of the report are 1024, the decommit gives back 1024 pages, a page committed again is zero, the report says `Protected`, `Uncommitted` and `Large`, the child stops on vector 14 in its guard page, each refusal has its status and an error record that is not `Refused`. The test build checks at the release of an uncommitted object that its committed pages are its slots with the bit, and the sums of the frames and the present pages of each mapping as before.
439. **Measured** (`kerneltool measure address_space`, only the desktop; the host had a load of 19 to 60 in every run, so the speeds are not valid by the rule of 8; three runs with the checks of the test build, two without). The arena: 1 GiB in one mapping paid 513 pages of frame list; the map of 1 GiB (its page tables, 2 MiB) took 3.7 to 9.8 ms (30.6 ms in one run). Pages committed and used in one second, with 1 / 16 / 256 ranges for each entry: 3 924 to 864 521 / 88 860 to 953 255 / 93 239 to 896 679 with the checks, 17 020 to 141 844 / 142 797 to 180 115 / 92 517 to 117 381 without: the load decides these numbers. The decommit of the sparse table: 1 268 to 1 822 us for 1 GiB of slots. One protection change of 64 pages with its shoot-down, in the middle of 200: 2 776 to 3 411 ns with 1 processor running the program, 2 822 to 9 639 ns with 2, 16 865 to 23 447 ns with 4: each other processor that runs the program costs its interrupt and its reload of the page table. One random read over 64 MiB: 13.4 to 36.1 ns through large pages, 12.4 to 52.3 ns through pages of 4 KiB of the same object: under KVM the guest walks are short when the host backs the guest with large pages, and these runs show no difference that the load does not hide.
440. **Corrections after the independent read of the virtual memory** (each with a case of `address_space` that failed on the kernel before its correction: the six were run on the kernel before and gave WRONG, then right). F1: a decommit wakes each thread of the program that waits on a word of a frame that returns, before the frame goes to the frame cache. F2: `word_address` on a large page keeps the bits 12 to 20 of the address. F3: an object whose pages have marks of protection is not mapped again and not given (`marked_pages`, kind `Object_Marked`); a part that gets the protection of its mapping has no mark. W1: a fault in a large mapping that its entry permits now (an old translation before a shoot-down) runs the instruction again; corrected by reading only, with no test (see the gaps). W2: an uncommitted object pays at least one page (a budget with no page cannot hold a slot of the table of memory objects). W3: `frames_take_aligned` moves its candidate above a used frame; one call changes at most 65 536 pages (`Too_Long` with its record; the longest decommit call is logged). W4: a part cannot get more than its mapping has now (`Bad_Operation`, kind `Bad_Value`); a protection above what the mapping was made with is `Bad_Operation` too (it was `No_Right` with a `Refused` record). Notes: a page that is not committed stops its program with the code `STOP_CODE_NOT_COMMITTED` (0x300) and the sentence "page fault on a page that is not committed"; a range of 0 pages is `Too_Small`; a protection of a part of device memory, of large pages or of a thread area is kind `Whole_Only`; an object held with no mapping can be decommitted; the attribute `@(private = "file")` of `read_memory_pages` is back on its procedure.
441. **Who pays a commit** (owner, 2026-11-11, "budget delegation", and the amendment of the coordinator): `Memory_Commit` takes a budget handle that must be the budget of the object, with the new right `Commit` (narrow: it makes nothing) or `Spend`; the right Write on the object no longer spends the budget of its maker. The ten cases of `ARCHITECTURE.md` ("Who pays a commit") run in `address_space` with a second program, each with its error record; new kinds `Not_Payer`, `Object_Shared`, `Object_Marked`, `Whole_Only`. Verified: a revocation of a budget handle does not end an object that was made with it.
442. **The addresses that the kernel chooses are used again** (the fault found by the agent of the tables): the first gap between the mappings, in place of a counter that only grew. `address_space` maps and unmaps one object 100 000 times: its budget holds the same pages after as after the first time. The run before the correction was not made in this step: the other agent's call host refused its 61st call.
443. **Corrections after the second read of the virtual memory.** B1 (blocking): an Uncommitted object of 1 to 4 pages paid one page that `reserved_pages` did not count, and the next report stopped the test machine; that page is a reserved page now (taken back at the release). F1': the wake of a decommit, and of a revocation (`origin.odin`, older than this step), also reaches a thread that registered its words and does not wait yet: it gets `woken`, and its `wait_end` does not wait (`thread_words_wake`); corrected by reading only: a test needs a decommit between the registration and the wait of another thread. F3': `unmap` ends the marks of protection of its pages, so the object can be mapped and given again. F4: the page limit of `Memory_Protect` counts one entry for each large page; a whole mapping of 4 KiB pages above 256 MiB is refused (a program that names its object alone changes it in parts); corrected by reading only: the test machine has no 256 MiB of large pages to give. W5: the wake of a decommit sorts the frames of the words and searches each page. W6: the error record of a commit reads at most 65 536 pages of the table. W7: an object of large pages mapped at an address that the kernel chooses gets an address on 2 MiB, and large pages. Notes: one table that names one mapping whole and in part is refused; `chosen_address` stops below `MODULE_BASE`; a `Memory_Map` into a stopped program names the state before the marks. Each case that a program can make is in `address_space` (B1, F3', W7, the table), and failed on the kernel before (B1 stopped the machine: "the frames do not add up"). The picture of `address_space`: the shoot-down shows the least and the most of five batches, since the program cannot know the load of the host.
444. **Corrections after the third read of the virtual memory.** The comments and the names of the cases of `address_space` say what each case does, with no review label ("a small object with no frame keeps the sums of the frames"), as the rule of the owner on comments asks; the cases are its "edge cases". A revocation that ends a mapping wakes the threads with a word in a frame of the object with one sorted search over the frames of the object (`waiters_on_frames_wake`, shared with the decommit), not one walk of the object for each word: with an Uncommitted object of 1 GiB, about 5 x 10^8 slot loads became one pass, which ends at the last frame of the object. `unmap` walks one table of the last level at a time, skips a range with no table, and stops after the last present entry of the mapping (the test build walks the whole mapping, to compare the count with the walk). `region_release` stops after the last frame of the object, and the test build checks the committed pages before it takes the frame lock. A whole protection change of a mapping of 4 KiB pages counts its present pages against the limit of one call, and walks its entries in the same way: a mapping of 1 GiB of an object that several programs name can change its protection whole. The error record of a protection that the limit refuses counts as the kernel counts (a large page is one entry). The comment of `thread_words_wake` says that the words can be dropped at the start of the next entry with no program lock, and why that is harmless. `Not_Multiple` (36) is after `Whole_Only` (35). Two cases of `address_space` were added: a revocation of a mapping of 1 GiB with a thread of the child that waits on a word of it (the waiter wakes at once; the time of the revocation is logged), and an Uncommitted object of 1 GiB with 4 frames that ends (each page returns; the time is logged). None of this was built or run by the author: a new rule of the owner gives the build and the tests to the coordinator.
445. **A version is the state of one object at one commit**, and a snapshot of a store is the versions of its objects at one generation: one structure serves both (`contract/store`, format `STORAGE2`). Each object names a version index (one block, up to 254 leaves); a leaf holds 85 version entries of 48 bytes: 21590 versions for one object. A table has 39 objects (it had 48): the entry of an object grows to 104 bytes with its time, its date and its index. The disk format changed: a disk of `STORAGE1` is not read (no backward compatibility, the owner's rule).
446. **Each commit that changes an object makes a version, with no call of the program.** What it costs, exactly: the bytes of the version are on the disk already (copy on write never wrote over them); the commit keeps them in place of making them free. It writes 2 more blocks (the leaf and the index) and reads none after the first commit of the object (a cache of 16 blocks of the service, found by number and check). A block of data whose bytes did not change is named again, not written again (one read to compare its bytes). So a key in `notes` (one block of data) writes 6 blocks (data, list, leaf, index, table, root) where it wrote 4. A version holds its list and its blocks that no other state names (`own_blocks`): 2 blocks for a key of the note, 4 for a step of the count (its 3 blocks change at each step). The time of 1000 commits of the count: 3224 ms recorded at step 31; 1736 ms and 3327 ms in two runs of this step on a host where other machines ran: the cost in time is below the noise of the host.
447. **The blocks of a store are its distinct blocks**, and the limit of the grant counts the versions: a shared block counts one time. The service keeps a reference count of each block in use (16 bits) and the store that names it; the open makes them again from the disk. A block named from two stores, or a count of a store that is not its distinct blocks, is a fault of the engine on the log (new counts of the open).
448. **Thinning at the limit is in the commit of the change**: the oldest versions of the store (by generation, in any object) go until the change fits. A loss of power never keeps a thinning without its change (the second loss of power of 38b falls on such a commit). A change that the current objects alone cannot hold is refused before any thinning: a refused change destroys no history. Its error record: Over_Limit, "with no earlier version kept".
449. **The retention rule** (`support/storage_retention`, host tests): each version is kept 10 minutes; then the newest of each hour until one day; then the newest of each day. An hour and a day are counted from the format, so a kept version stays kept. The service applies it to an object at a commit of that object, at most one time a minute. The times are the dates of the versions when the system has a date (the time since the format does not count the time when no machine ran); a version with no date keeps its age in the time since the format (the root keeps it; the service adds its own time).
450. **Five exports**, each with its description and error records: `versions` (a Table), `version`, `restore` (a commit whose object names the list of the version: no block of data is written; the state before becomes a version), `compare` (a Table of byte ranges; two equal pointers are equal bytes and are not read), `clone` (the new object names the same list). Their first argument names the store: empty for the store of the caller; another store needs a caller that acts for the person (No_Grant Person, -10), as the console and the remote service do. A remove ends an object and its versions.
451. **`notes` has undo to any version with no code to keep them**: Up and Down step through the versions of the note (it reads `versions` and `version`), Enter restores the version shown, Escape goes back. The program commits each key, as before: the measure of one commit of the note is about 4 ms in the machine test.
452. **The crash search checks the versions**: each image gives each object and each of its versions, byte for byte, as the durable image or as the image with every write. A second loss of power falls during a thinning at the limit. The tool keeps the check of each block of the durable image that no pending write touched: the 306 images of a disk with 1119 versions take 6 s, not 15 minutes.
453. **The real-time clock is read by a program, `date`**, which receives the two ports and the date page with Write: a driver is a program, and the root service keeps no clock code. It reads the clock at the edge of a second, and its service gives `now()`.
454. **The date lives in a page that each program maps read-only** (`contract/date`, the context value 15), on the model of the settings page: the date of the time 0 of the system. A program adds the time of the time page: no kernel entry, no call. A program that starts before `date` wrote the page reads no date, and the bar shows the time since boot until then.
455. **UTC, no leap second, a correction of drift each 10 s**: the time zone is a field of the page that no setting writes yet; a day has 86400 seconds. The first full check found the date 22 s behind the host after 5 minutes on a loaded host (the time of the system, from the cycle counter, ran slow), with a date read one time: `date` now reads the clock again each 10 s and writes the page again, and the log gives each move of the date. The date of a version replaces its time since the format for the retention rule, so a machine that was off for a day ages its versions by a day.
456. **`kerneltool test tcp`** runs the two TCP machines alone, as `test power` and `test main` run theirs.
457. **The corrections of the independent read of the versions and the date** (written, not yet built or run by the author). The counts of names of a block are 32 bits, and a commit that would pass that type, or take a count below 0, is refused; the second case is a fault of the engine, and the store takes no change until the next start. A damaged version index or leaf no longer locks its object: the next commit of the object drops those versions. A full version index compacts its leaves. The open takes its recount of each store as the count of the store, and marks damaged an index, a leaf or a state whose count the format does not permit; a fault of the engine found at the open stops the changes of its store. The age of a version is the smaller of its age by the date and its age by running time. A reader of the date page gives up after 64 reads. The clock is decoded with each field checked, a window of 2020 to 2099 on the year, and a bound on each read. One commit refuses more than 65536 changes of counts or 1024 writes with its own error (Full, unit Commit_Blocks). The disk format does not change: the counts are in memory only. Host tests: `support/storage_mount` (a hostile disk, the recount, a count above 65535, the compaction and a model of a long run and of 30000 restores), `support/storage_retention` (the ages), `support/calendar` (the clock), `contract/date` (a writer stopped in a change).
458. **A line record in the draw table** (`Line`, draw records version 4, the byte `reserved` becomes `line_thickness`). The pixels are those of `graphics.line`, and a line is always stored from its left end, so a line and its reverse draw the same pixels. The damage is the bounding rectangle: pieces of the path would be joined again by the damage list, because they touch. A thickness of 0 gives no record; a thickness past 32 is cut to 32; an end past 32 768 pixels gives no record. `rings` uses it. `threads`, `workers` and `processors` show the pixels of their threads with a picture record. `crowd` and `notes` lose a margin and an empty band.
459. **The corrections of the independent read of the interface package.**
- A dragged window keeps 8 pixels of its title on the screen at each edge; a window wider than the screen starts with its close button on the screen (`support/bar/window_place.odin`).
- A new font reaches both pictures of a surface: `draw_whole_window_next`, also in the terminal and the manual.
- The compositor uses a surface handle only when the kernel proves that the client gave it through its own ring (`Handle_State` with the ring memory), and closes it on each return. The kernel already had the proof: no change of the kernel or of a contract.
- The present is the whole surface when the table of the shown picture is not known. A lost present makes the next frame of its picture whole.
- Each edge of a record is cut, then its size is computed. `refused` is apart from `full`. `grid_valid` cannot wrap.
- The keys hint of the terminal does not run over its status, and the status bar cannot wrap.
460. **The other primitives come from the same origin, with the same proof**: Poly1305, ChaCha20-Poly1305, SHA-2, HMAC, HKDF, X25519, Ed25519 and AES-128-GCM, 37 files in all. Where the origin chooses a hardware implementation by the processor (`core:sys/info`), a file of the system takes the place of that choice with the procedures of the generic file of the origin, unchanged: the portable code runs. `compatibility_hash` takes the place of `core:crypto/hash` with SHA-2 only. `HAS_RAND_BYTES` is false: a key comes from `support/random`.
461. **The cryptography is the module `cryptography`.** Its exports have long names (`seal_with_chacha20_poly1305`). The caller supplies each byte, and a failure is an error record. A tag or a signature that does not verify gives the new error kind `Not_Authentic` (32): no byte is given, and the count is negative, so no caller can take it for a success. Each copy of a secret in the module is erased before the export returns. The code runs with a zero `context`: `runtime.default_context()` stores the default random generator of the runtime, whose state is thread-local, which a module does not have. One call of an export costs 4 to 12 ns, a seal of 1200 bytes 2.4 us (ChaCha20-Poly1305) or 11.2 us (AES-128-GCM).
462. **The seam of the batches.** The batch exports wait for the tables as parameters of `work/tables`. Until then `support/quic` calls the cryptography through one procedure table of its own, so that a batch export later changes that table only.
463. **The corrections of the read of the cryptography** (`review_cryptography.md`). F1: an open read the sealed text two times (the tag check, then the decryption), so a writer of that memory got a forged text accepted; the open now copies the sealed text into the result once and opens in place, with the tag in the module. F2: `ed25519_sign` hashed the message two times and read R back from the result; it now signs a copy of the message (4096 bytes at most) into the module. Tests: `an_open_while_another_thread_changes_the_sealed_text` and `a_signature_while_another_thread_changes_the_message` (`modules/cryptography`, a second thread flips a byte during 200 000 opens and 20 000 signatures). The other copies (nonce, additional data, info of HKDF, outputs) and the headers read once at the entry (W5); an open into a result with no room is `Too_Small` (W2); a result that overlaps an argument with an offset is `Bad_Value` (W3); X25519 gives `Bad_Value` for a point of small order (N3); `erase_stack_below_the_caller` erases 16 KiB of stack after each export, a refill and a hash of a key (W4); one generator for each thread, with a check that fails closed (W6); the strength of the seed goes with each seed, in the context value 15 (W7); the check of origin walks the folders and checks the files of the system by their hashes (W1); root stops with a sentence if its seed is zeros (N7).
464. **QUIC packet protection is a library in the program, over the module `cryptography`** (`docs/QUIC.md`). `support/quic` reads a header as hostile input, derives the Initial keys from the Destination Connection ID, derives packet keys from a traffic secret, seals and opens a packet in place, applies header protection, and checks a Retry integrity tag. It calls the primitives through `quic.Cryptography`, the procedures of the module that the program fills from its import slots: the one seam where batch exports will come. Each datagram is copied once into private memory before any byte of it is read. Two exports join the module for header protection: `header_protection_mask_with_aes_128` and `header_protection_mask_with_chacha20`. AES-128-GCM protects the Initial packets, as RFC 9001 asks; ChaCha20-Poly1305 is the preferred suite after the handshake, because its portable code is 4.6 times faster here. No kernel change is needed for QUIC. The tests are RFC 9001 appendix A (A.1 to A.5) and RFC 9000 appendix A, whose values a script copied from the text of the RFCs, and hostile datagrams.
465. **A program chooses the least seed strength that it accepts** (owner, 2026-11-11: "je pense que les fonctions qu'on expose devrait permettre de choisir ce que le programme tolère et dans le cas où les requis que le programme attend ne sont pas satisfaits et ben dans ce cas là c'est une erreur c'est pas grave"). `random.fill_for_a_key` takes the least strength (weak, processor, entropy) and gives the new error kind `Seed_Too_Weak` (33) and no byte when the seed of the program is weaker. A plain draw stays as it is. The module `cryptography` draws no random byte. QUIC and TLS ask for processor at least.
466. **TLS 1.3 for QUIC, with raw public keys** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 4). Both sides as one state machine over bytes of the caller, with no allocation: X25519 and Ed25519 only; ChaCha20-Poly1305 first and AES-128-GCM also; one ALPN; the transport parameters as bytes; no resumption, no 0-RTT, no HelloRetryRequest, no client certificate. The server proves an Ed25519 key in a Certificate that holds its SubjectPublicKeyInfo only (RFC 7250); the client compares it with the key that its caller pinned: no X.509, no certificate authority, no date. Each message of the peer is hostile input, and each failure gives the alert of TLS and an error record. The key schedule is tested against RFC 8448, section 3.
467. **The corrections of the second read of the cryptography** (`review_cryptography_second.md`). B1: a ChaCha20 header protection sample that starts with `ff ff ff ff` asked the origin for the block at the counter 0xffffffff, which its limit check refused, and the program stopped; the coordinator makes one listed adjustment of the copied `_chacha20/chacha20.odin` (the last block is allowed), and the tests cover that block and that sample. F3: `erase_stack_below_the_caller` writes from the top down, so a short stack stops at its guard page before any other mapping is written; a thread that calls the module or draws random bytes needs 24 KiB of stack. W10: the proof of origin refuses a symbolic link and a file that no list can name. W11: the module takes 2048 bytes of additional data (a header with its token). W12: the open of a packet has two steps, so that the caller chooses the keys from the key phase. W14: the seal writes the packet number and checks the Length. W15 and W9: the missing tests. W13 (the size of the erasure) waits for a measure by the coordinator.
468. **The transport of one QUIC connection** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 5). A `Connection` record in memory of the caller, with no allocation: the three packet number spaces, ACK frames, loss detection by the packet and time thresholds and the probe timeout of RFC 9002, a fixed congestion window of 12 000 bytes (NewReno comes after), flow control, one bidirectional stream that the client opens, the idle timeout, the anti-amplification limit of a server, the closing and draining states, and the handshake driven by the TLS state machine. Each frame of the peer is hostile input, with the error code of RFC 9000. NEW_CONNECTION_ID, RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID, RESET_STREAM and STOP_SENDING are minimal; Version Negotiation, Retry, key update and the stateless reset are not done. The tests run two connections in memory over a channel that the test controls.
469. **The corrections of the third read of QUIC** (`review_quic_third.md`). F5: a packet whose data has no place in a range set is not acknowledged, so the peer sends the data again; a sent packet whose data has no place stays in flight. F6: a server takes a client only with an Initial that opens with keys derived into local records, and a handshake that does not complete within 10 s ends the record. W16: a floor of packet numbers, under which each packet is refused. W17: times saturate, and the ack delay of the peer is at most its max_ack_delay. W19: the transcript holds 8192 bytes. W20: handshake bytes at a level whose keys were replaced are a PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. N15 and N17: `tls_start` and the start of a connection refuse a least seed strength under processor, and a client with no pinned key. W18 (the size of the stack erasure) waits for the measure of the coordinator; `docs/QUIC.md` says where a `Connection` record lives.
470. **The table of error details** (owner: "l'idée d'une table de détails c'est exactement ce que j'avais en tête c'est optionnel"): an error record stays 56 bytes, and a caller that wants each thing that went wrong gives an optional Append table of error records, the last parameter of an export that has an error record (`MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, section 8). An error detail is an error record, so each sentence writes it; the error record of the call is the first detail, so a caller with no table receives the record of before. A callee with `nil` stops at the first problem, as before: one test of a pointer. The three ways use the Append table as it is; after a failure the caller copies back the details and no other table. The loader gives load errors as details (its own form): each import that is missing or has the wrong hash. The call from the host carries the details in the bytes of a failed answer (`Request.details_end`, `Answer.details`), and the tool reads whole records only, at most the count. Host tests: the same details in the direct and the isolated way, a table too small keeps the count, the details of a load in its order, a count of the answer that lies. The machine test: `monitor.counts_named` with two rows that name nothing gives "detail 2 of 2", and `imports` loads `lonely` with a table.
NEW-1. **A listen grant of UDP** (`docs/NETWORK.md`, "The listen grant of UDP"). The datagram contract had a listen grant for TCP only. A datagram endpoint can now listen on one local UDP port that the root service names, and answer the remotes that came to it, with the table of admitted remotes of TCP unchanged. A new remote is admitted on a datagram of 1200 bytes at least (`LISTEN_FIRST_LEAST`, the least first datagram of a QUIC client), from an address that can answer, when a Receive waits. No kernel change.
NEW-2. **A QUIC server of several connections** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `support/quic/server.odin`). A table of 16 connection records in a mapping of the program. A datagram goes to the record of its Destination Connection ID and its remote; an Initial with a new identifier takes a record only when a copy of it opens, and under the limits: 8 connections and 2 handshakes for one remote address, 8 handshakes at once. A datagram over a limit gets no answer. A long header of another version gets one Version Negotiation packet, 16 in one second at most. The server sends `disable_active_migration`. Not done: Retry, the stateless reset.
NEW-3. **`quic_echo`, and the key of a server** (`programs/quic_echo`). An echo server over the listen grant of UDP 4433, with the module `cryptography` through its imports. It makes its Ed25519 key at its start from its own generator (strength "processor" at least), writes its public key on the log and in its window for a client to pin, and keeps the private key in its memory; a new start makes a new key. A weak seed makes no key and no endpoint, and the window says so.
NEW-4. **The QUIC client of the peer, and `kerneltool test quic`** (`tools/kerneltool/peer_quic.odin`, `quic_scenario.odin`). The peer links `support/quic` and the module compiled for the host, pins the key that the machine printed (step Quic_Key), and runs cases (step Peer_Quic): 1 MiB each way, with loss, reorder and duplicates, 8 clients, a wrong pinned key, a flood of 1000 forged Initials from 198.18.0.0/15 with a datagram of another version, and a client that vanishes. A stack of another project (OpenSSL 3.6 `s_client -quic -enable_server_rpk`) and `remote` over QUIC are designed, not built (`docs/QUIC.md`, sections 6 and 7).
NEW-5. **Retry: a server keeps nothing before a client proved its address** (`review_quic_fourth.md`, F7; `docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `support/quic/retry.odin`). An Initial with no token gets a Retry, and no record, no key, no open of the Initial. The token is ChaCha20-Poly1305 under a key that the server makes at its start, over the original identifier of the client, the identifier of the Retry and a time, with the address and port of the client as additional data; it lives 10 s. A record is taken only for an Initial whose token opens for its remote and names its identifier. A client takes one Retry, checks its tag, and checks `original_destination_connection_id` and `retry_source_connection_id`. A replay of a token works only from the address and port of its client, for 10 s.
NEW-6. **Budgets of the work of new identifiers** (F8). In each second: 2000 Retries (2 exports each), 2000 checks of a token (1 export), 500 checks of an Initial with a valid token (11 exports): about 41 ms of one processor in the worst second. Over a budget, a datagram is dropped before any cryptography. The server copies each datagram once and decides on that copy (W23); `quic_echo` gives the datagrams of known records first in each pass.
NEW-7. **The network service holds the limit of 3 times** (W21; `contract/datagram`, `programs/network`). A UDP listen grant sends to a remote at most 3 times the bytes that came from it, until the program marks it with the new command `Validate` (status `Amplification` else); a send no longer keeps a place alive. A program that validates every remote lifts the limit for the addresses that sent it a datagram, for 120 s after their last datagram.
NEW-8. **The other points of the fourth read**: a NAT that changes the address or the port of a client ends its connection (W22, written in `docs/QUIC.md`); a record has a generation number for the state of its program (N23); the closing state answers the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th... datagram (N25); the tests send the flood of F7 (N28). `kerneltool test quic-real` runs `openssl s_client -quic` of the host against `quic_echo` through QEMU user networking, written and not run.
NEW-9. **The UDP relay of the peer for `kerneltool test quic-real`** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `tools/kerneltool/peer_relay.odin`). QEMU user networking with `restrict=on` drops each UDP datagram of the machine (libslirp, `udp_input`), and `restrict=on` stays. The machine of `test quic-real` has the network device of the peer, and the peer relays UDP between a socket of the loopback of the host and the UDP port 4433 of the machine, from 10.0.2.2 and a port for each client of the host (a table of 16). `peer_run` reads every frame that waits before the next sends of the cases. `quic_echo` logs the error code, the frame type and the reason phrase of each close. The test needs only the loopback of the host: it can join the whole test after a first pass.

## Gaps

- **A fault at the return to a program is a fault of the kernel** (Review 10): the kernel refuses the one known cause (a thread whose entry or stack is not an address of a program), but any other state of a thread that the processor refuses at `iretq` would stop the machine. To do: treat a fault at that instruction as a fault of the program.

Against the properties and the module contract:

- Property 19 is not complete: the description has no floats, no text of a variable size in a record, no record in a record, and no handle in a result.
- Tables as parameters (417 to 428): the console cannot call an export with an Append table or with Bytes that change (the language has no second result), and `kerneltool call` gives tables to read only. A record of a table has no Handle. The direct call checks nothing: a caller in the same address space that gives a count larger than its memory makes the export read past it, as with any address; only the isolated call checks. In a lent memory object the callee can write each byte of the object while the call runs, not only the table: the granularity is the object; only a call host takes this form, a service refuses it. The receiver checks overlap between parameters, not between the records of one parameter that name bytes of another (two packets of `seal` on the same bytes). The call region is shared during the call: an export that checks a value of a record and uses it later copies it first (as for a Text, choice 186). The console starts call hosts with its own code, not with `system.call_host_start`. The kernel gives each mapping that chooses no address a new range and never reuses one: a program that maps memory at each call with no address grows its page tables until its budget refuses (421 works around it in the receiver; the kernel is not changed; the fault is given to the agent of `work/scheduler`). The measures of 427 ran at a load of the host of 32 to 48.
- `interface_module` refuses an element larger than its element frame: 64 draw records or 4096 bytes of text in one element call.
- The interface package is a module for one program only (`interface_through_module`); the elements that take input (a button, a list, a text input) are not exports: they need the interface state of the program as a table of one row with the direction Change.
- Information functions: the catalog is the boot image and the replacement table: the module store does not exist. The error record of a malformed module gives the kind of the problem and its byte; `problem(name)` gives the sentence. `manual` reads the module records with the machine and the right Inspect, which also shows the state of each program: no grant gives the module records only. A row holds a hash as 64 hexadecimal digits, 32 bytes more than the hash; a signature longer than 112 bytes ends with "...". A function that answers about all the modules checks each file each time, with no cache: the measure includes it. The functions trust the hash of the entry of the boot image: they do not compute the hash of each file. The console keeps 256 KiB of tables for one line and 32 KiB in a variable, and a record that `join` makes has 256 bytes at most: `columns` chooses fewer fields. `where` compares for equality only. The module records come from the kernel, so `running()` shows a module that a program maps by itself only if its loader wrote a record (gap of choice 165).
- Imports and replacements: the replacement table binds the loaders of the system: the root service, the console, the call host. A program that maps code by itself can ignore it: the kernel does not check what a program does with its own memory. A module record is what the loader asserts: a program writes and removes the records of its own program, and the kernel does not compare them with the memory. A change of the table does not reach a program that runs: the root service does not start a program again. The loader finds a module only in the boot image, and the person names a module of the table by its name there: the module store does not exist. The check cannot prove that the module of a replacement behaves as the module that it replaces, and no principal signs the table (P9). A module has 8 imports at most, a program 16 modules at most, the table 32 records. A load error holds the first failure only, and one replacement of the path; it does not say which field of two Records differs (see `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "The error of a load"). The root service writes the error of a start on the log only: the program that asked the start receives a status.
- A direct call does not limit a Handle to the rights of its description: the module is in the program of the caller and has each handle of that program.
- Revocation: a grant has no end time. The root service cannot revoke one ring (it closes its handle after the start): to end a connection it uses `Disconnect` or stops a program. The person sees the grants of a program in the console only: no window shows the grants of all the programs. A grant that the root service did not make (a handle that one program gave to another) is in the kernel table only: no command shows it.
- A caller can give handles through a call ring and never send the call: the handles fill the 128 places of the table of the receiver until the caller revokes them or stops. A service with several callers needs a limit for each caller.
- `program.has_ports` stays true after the last Ports handle of a program closed: the change of program on a processor then reads the handle table for nothing.
- The console makes a handle only with `buffer`. It cannot make a handle with a part of the rights of another handle.
- A service: the root service has a list of the services that a program can call only for a program with the grant Connect (`callable_of`, today `remote`); the console connects to each service. A caller finds the file of a service only in the boot image. A call that never returns stops the one thread of a service such as `paint`: the person stops the call in the console (the console closes the connection) and closes the window of the service.
- A terminal shows one program, and a program has one terminal. A program that has a terminal has no surface. The terminal gives keys only: no pointer, no paste, no size of the view. The person cannot select or copy text. The terminal shows bytes as characters of one cell: no Unicode beyond the font, no wide character. The terminal of a stopped program stays until the person closes its window: no command of the console closes it.
- Only `terminal` has two pictures: the other programs can still show a part of a new picture with a part of the old one.
- The kernel ring of a thread has 32 records. `workers` still takes its tiles from a queue with a lock, not from a shared queue.
- A thread that stops in the middle of a write or a read of a shared queue stops the queue: no thread repairs the record of a thread that is not there.
- The milliseconds of the time page change at a tick, each millisecond; a finer time is `system.nanoseconds()`, from the cycle counter (choice 250). A deadline of a thread still has the resolution of the tick. No check compares the cycle counters of two processors. The time page has no date: the kernel does not read the clock of the machine.
- Limits: the root service gives each program the processor time of all its threads: no program of the desktop is limited below what it can use, and no command changes a limit while a program runs. A thread that another processor stops loses the count of its last part of a time slice (less than 1 ms). The scheduler passes over a thread with no time left each time it looks: with many such threads it reads them for nothing. The log limit is for each second on the tick: 100 records at the change of second can pass as two times the limit.
- A program can write, change and remove its own module records (`Program_Module` with program 0): the records that its starter wrote are not protected from it, and a report does not say who wrote a record. Found by the coordinator at the merge of step 34. Remove: the kernel marks each record with its writer (the program itself, or a holder of its handle), and a program cannot change a record of its starter.
- Error records (choices 227 to 241): the kernel finds the record of an operation after it failed, from the operation table, not from the check that refused. An operation whose checks change without a change of the table gives a wrong argument, or `Refused`; the program `errors` tests 10 kernel cases, not each check of each operation. These refusals give `Refused` with the status: the ranges of `Device_Create`, `Memory_Physical` on memory that is not contiguous, `No_Memory`, and a `Bad_State` of `Handle_Give` through memory. A record holds the first argument that is wrong; a table of error details gives each one, but only `monitor.counts_named` and the loader (Find and Read) declare it, and a kernel operation has none (the design is in `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, section 8, "The table of error details": a bit of the kind of a command, not built, because it costs one instruction for each command).
